Homomorphisms to Hook Specht Modules over Quiver Hecke algebras of type C
Martín Forsberg Conde, Berta Hudak

Abstract
. We investigate homomorphisms between Specht modules for level 1 1 cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebras of affine type C C . For hook partitions with a ‘short leg’ or a ‘short arm’, we give the full quiver Hecke algebra action and we show that every non-zero homomorphism from an arbitrary Specht module is determined by sending the cyclic generator to a single standard basis element. We use this action to investigate homomorphisms to these Specht modules, giving a full classification in the short leg case. This provides the first investigation of homomorphisms between Specht modules in affine type C C and extends methods previously used in affine type A A .

†
†

2020 Mathematics subject classification: 20C08, 05E10 (Primary), 16G10, 17B37, 20C30 (Secondary)

1.
Introduction

The study of the structure of Specht modules is the central problem in the modular representation theory of symmetric groups and related algebras. In type A (and over a field
k
), the Brundan–Kleshchev isomorphism
[
4
]
provides good guidance to a mathematician working on quiver Hecke algebras. The representation theory of cyclotomic Hecke algebras (not to mention symmetric groups) is a much older subject, and the isomorphism immediately suggests that any result about Specht modules of cyclotomic Hecke algebras should be provable in the quiver Hecke algebra formalism. Work in this direction helps build intuition about quiver Hecke algebras in general, and reveals the (previously hidden) graded structure of cyclotomic Hecke algebras.

However, in type C, there is no analogous result to the Brundan–Kleshchev isomorphism. The general approach to studying quiver Hecke algebras of type C is to reason by analogy with type A, and find out the differences through computations. Ariki, Park, and Speyer
[
2
]
recently constructed Specht modules in type C, and Evseev and Mathas
[
6
]
showed that the algebras are graded cellular. There have also been studies on their representation type
[
3
,
5
,
1
]
.

In this paper, we focus on homomorphisms between Specht modules of type C. This problem has not been studied before, and only special cases can be solved in type A. To have any hope of reaching a result, we should restrict the conditions in some way. A non-trivial yet manageable restriction, motivated by results in type A, is to require that
\ell=1
and that the label
\lambda
of the target Specht module
S^{\lambda}
is a hook-shaped partition. In type A, James
[
9
]
gave a complete description of homomorphisms to the trivial module. Much later, Loubert
[
11
]
gave a complete description of homomorphisms to Specht modules labelled by a hook-shaped
\lambda
under the restriction
e>2
. A partial description of the situation when
e=2
was given by Hudak
[
8
]
. The results by Loubert and Hudak were proven using the new quiver Hecke algebra formalism. We will give a full description of homomorphisms to Specht modules labelled by hook-shaped partitions
\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})
which have a ‘short leg’ (that is,
b<2e
), and a partial description of the corresponding homomorphisms when
\lambda
has a ‘short arm’ (that is,
a<2e
). Under the short leg/arm restriction, the quiver Hecke algebra action is tractable, which allows us to give a full description of the homomorphisms in most cases.

Two key features of our description are:

(1)

The homomorphisms we describe map the Specht module generator
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}}
to a single basis element of
S^{\lambda}
, which follows from
section

3.1
.

(2)

All the homomorphisms that we find for short leg hook-shaped modules in type
C_{e}^{(1)}
have a counterpart in type
A_{2e-1}^{(1)}
, according to the Main Theorem of
[
11
]
, as we describe in
section

4.4
.

The structure of this text is as follows.
In
section

2
, we give the necessary background on quiver Hecke algebras of type
C_{e}^{(1)}
, their Specht modules, and string diagrams. We review the relevant tableaux combinatorics under the different residue structure that applies in type
C_{e}^{(1)}
.

In
section

3
, we describe the action of the quiver Hecke algebra on hook Specht modules with a short arm or short leg. This serves as our main technical tool, and it provides the corollary that all homomorphisms to these Specht modules send the Specht module generator to a single basis element.

In
section

4
, we present a full explicit description of the homomorphisms from an arbitrary Specht module
S^{\mu}
to a Specht module
S^{\lambda}
, where
\lambda
has a short leg.

In
section

5
, we discuss the analogous case where
\lambda
has a short arm. The case where the charge of the hook is
0
or
e
is especially hard in type
C
and we were not able to obtain a full characterization, so we limit the discussion to some examples.

2.
Background

2.1.
Lie theory notation

We follow
[
10
]
and use standard notation for the root datum.

Let
e\in\{2,3,\dots\}
and
I=\{0,1,2,\ldots,e\}
and suppose we are given a quiver of affine type
C_{e}^{(1)}
, as follows.

0
1
2
e{-}1
e

We assign integer labels in
I
from left to right to each vertex of the quiver.

Then we have the
simple roots

\Pi=\{\alpha_{i}\mid i\in I\}
and the
fundamental weights

\{\Lambda_{i}\mid i\in I\}
in the
weight lattice

P
.

There is a symmetric bilinear form
(-,-)
on
P
satisfying the following:

(1)

(\Lambda_{i},\alpha_{j})=d_{j}\delta_{ij}
; and

(2)

(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})=d_{i}a_{ij}
,

where
d=(2,1,\dots,1,2)
if
e<\infty
.
We call
Q:=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}\alpha_{i}
the
root lattice
and
Q^{+}=\bigoplus_{i\in I}\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\alpha_{i}
the
positive cone
of the root lattice.
For
\beta=\sum_{i\in i}a_{i}\alpha_{i}\in Q^{+}
, let
{\rm ht}(\beta)
denote the
height
of
\beta
, that is
{\rm ht}(\beta)=\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}
.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{n}=\langle s_{k}\mid k=1,\ldots,n-1\rangle
denote the symmetric group on
n
letters.
The group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts on the set
I^{n}
by place permutations.
If
\text{\boldmath$i$}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})\in I^{n}
, then its
weight
is
|\text{\boldmath$i$}|:=\alpha_{i_{1}}+\dots+\alpha_{i_{n}}\in Q^{+}
.

For our purposes, the quiver
C_{\infty}
can simply be taken to result from the limit
e\rightarrow\infty
in the above construction.

2.2.
Combinatorics

In this section, we recall some standard combinatorial notions.

A
partition
of
n\geq 0
is a weakly decreasing sequence of non-negative integers
\mu=(\mu_{1},\mu_{2},\dots)
such that
|\mu|=\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}+\dots=n
.
If
\mu
is a partition of
n
, we write
\mu\vdash n
.
We denote the set of partitions of
n
by
\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
For any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, its
Young diagram
is defined as the set

[\mu]=\{(r,c)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\times\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}\mid c\leq\mu_{r}\}.

For
\mu\vdash n
, we say that
\mu
is a
partition of hook shape
(or simply a
hook
) if
\mu_{i}\leq 1
for all
i\geq 2
.
In this paper, we are concerned with a special shape of hooks:

Definition 2.1
.

A short leg (resp. arm) hook is a partition
\mu=(a+1,1^{b})
such that
b<2e
(resp.
a<2e
).

We define
\overline{\mkern 2.0mu\cdot\mkern 2.0mu}:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow I
by
k\mapsto|k|
if
e=\infty
and, otherwise, by

(1)

\displaystyle\overline{0+2e\mathbb{Z}}=0,\quad\overline{e+2e\mathbb{Z}}=e,

\displaystyle\overline{k+2e\mathbb{Z}}=\overline{2e-k+2e\mathbb{Z}}=k\quad\text{ for }1\leq k\leq e-1.

Note that this function is periodic.

Given a
charge

\kappa\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define
\Lambda_{\kappa}\in P^{+}
by
\Lambda_{\kappa}:=\Lambda_{\overline{\kappa}}.

If
\mu\vdash n
, we associate to any node
A=(r,c)\in[\mu]
its
content
defined by

\cont A=\kappa+c-r

and its
residue
defined by

\res A=\overline{\kappa+c-r}.

An
i
-node is a node with residue
i
.

Example 2.2
.

If
\mu=(7,4,4,3,2,2)
,
\kappa=1
and
e=3
, then
\mu
has the following residue pattern.

1
2
3
2
1
0
1
0
1
2
3
1
0
1
2
2
1
0
3
2
2
3

We call a node
A\in[\mu]

removable
(resp.
addable
) if
[\mu]\setminus A
(resp.
[\mu]\cup A
) is a valid Young diagram for a partition of
n-1
(resp.
n+1
).
For an
i
-node
A\in[\mu]
, we set

d_{A}(\mu)=d_{i}\cdot(\#\{\text{addable $i$-nodes of $[\mu]$ below }A\}-\#\{\text{removable $i$-nodes of $[\mu]$ below }A\}).

(Recall that
d=(2,1\dots,1,2)
.)

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
A
\mu
-tableau
is a bijection
{\mathfrak{t}}:[\mu]\rightarrow\{1,\dots,n\}
.
We depict
{\mathfrak{t}}
by filling each node
(r,c)\in[\mu]
with
{\mathfrak{t}}(r,c)
.
We say that the tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}
is
standard
if its entries increase along rows and down the columns and denote the set of standard tableaux by
\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
.

The symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
acts on the set of
\mu
-tableaux on the right by its natural action on the entries of the tableaux.
Let
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
be the unique
\mu
-tableau in which the numbers
1,2,\dots,n
appear in order from left to right along the successive rows, working from top to bottom.
We call
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
the
(row) initial tableau
. The initial tableau gives an ordering of the nodes of the Young diagram of a partition
\mu
, so that we write
N<M
if
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N)<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(M)
.
Set

\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}:=\text{\boldmath$i$}^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}}.

For every
\mu
-tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}
, we define the permutation
w^{\mathfrak{t}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
by

{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}w^{\mathfrak{t}}={\mathfrak{t}}.

We also define, for each
\mu
-tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}
, its
content sequence

\displaystyle\bm{c}({\mathfrak{t}})

\displaystyle=(c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n})

\displaystyle=(\cont{{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(1)},\ \cont{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(2),\dots,\cont{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(n)).

and its
residue sequence

\displaystyle\text{\boldmath$i$}({\mathfrak{t}})

\displaystyle=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})

\displaystyle=(\res{{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(1)},\ \res{{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(2)},\dots,\res{{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(n)}).

We write
\text{\boldmath$i$}^{\mu}
for the residue sequence of the initial tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
. We write
\ell(\bm{i})
for the length of the residue sequence
\bm{i}
.

Let
0\leq m\leq n
.
For a
\mu
-tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}
, we denote by
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
the set of nodes of
[\mu]
whose entries are less than or equal to
m
.
If
{\mathfrak{t}}
is standard, then
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
is also a standard tableau for some partition.

For any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\mu)
, we define the
degree

\deg{\mathfrak{t}}
of
{\mathfrak{t}}
inductively.
If
n=0
then
{\mathfrak{t}}
is the unique empty tableau and we set
\deg\mathtt{T}:=0
.
Otherwise, let
A={\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(n)\in[\mu]
and suppose
A
is an
i
-node. We set

\displaystyle\deg{\mathfrak{t}}:=\deg{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow n-1}+d_{A}(\mu).

Example 2.3
.

If
\mu=(3,3,2,1)
,
\kappa=1
and
e=2
, then
\mu
has the following residue pattern

1

2

1

0

1

2

1

0

2

and if
\mathtt{T}
is the tableau

1

3

4

2

5

8

6

9

7

then

\deg\mathtt{T}=0+0+0+(1+1)+1+0+0-2+0=1.

2.3.
KLR algebras of type C

Let us work over a field
k
of characteristic
p
. For simplicity, let us assume
e<\infty
. We set polynomials
\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)\in k[u,v]
satisfying
\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)=\mathcal{Q}_{j,i}(v,u)
, for
i,j\in I
, of the form

(2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)=\begin{cases}u+v^{2}&\text{if }(i,j)=(0,1),\\
u+v&\text{if }i\neq 0,j=i+1,j\neq\ell,\\
u^{2}+v&\text{if }(i,j)=(\ell-1,\ell),\\
1&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

following
[
2
, eq (2.1)]
. The algebra
R_{n}
associated with the polynomials
\mathcal{Q}_{i,j}(u,v)
is defined to be the unital associative diagrammatic
k
-algebra with generators

(3)

The summands conserving the crossings in the right-hand side of
eq.

5
and
eq.

6
are termed
Slide
terms, while the rest of the terms are called
Error
terms.

Finally, the
e(\bm{i})
form a set of mutually orthogonal idempotents.

(7)

e(\bm{i})e(\bm{j})=\delta_{\bm{i},\bm{j}}e(\bm{i}),\qquad\sum_{\bm{i}\in I^{n}}e(\bm{i})=1.

The algebra
R_{n}
is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded by setting

(8)

\deg(e(\bm{i}))=0,\qquad\deg(y_{r}e(\bm{i}))=(\alpha_{i_{r}},\alpha_{i_{r}})=d_{i_{r}},\qquad\deg(\psi_{s}e(\bm{i}))=-(\alpha_{i_{s}},\alpha_{i_{s+1}})

for all admissible
r,s
and
\bm{i}
.

For each element
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
, we fix a preferred reduced expression
w=s_{k_{1}}\dots s_{k_{m}}
and define the corresponding element of
R_{n}
by

(9)

\psi_{w}=\psi_{s_{k_{1}}}\dots\psi_{s_{k_{m}}}.

Note that unless
w\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is fully commutative,
\psi_{w}
depends on this choice of reduced expression.

For
\Lambda\in P^{+}
, we can define the algebra
R^{\Lambda}_{n}
as the quotient of
R_{n}
by the additional cyclotomic relations
y_{1}^{\langle\alpha^{\vee}_{i_{1}},\Lambda\rangle}e(\bm{i})=0
for
\bm{i}\in I^{n}
to obtain the cyclotomic quiver Hecke algebra.

2.4.
Specht modules

The graded (row) Specht modules over quiver Hecke algebras of type
C
were described in
[
2
]
. The simple Specht relations are the same as in type A.

The residue relation is also similar.

(11)

v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\bm{\lambda}}}e(\bm{i})=\delta_{\bm{i},\bm{i}^{\bm{\lambda}}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\bm{\lambda}}}.

The homogeneous Garnir relations in type
C_{e}^{(1)}
were conjectured in
[
2
, Conjecture 5.3]
to be the same as the Garnir relations in type
A_{2e-1}^{(1)}
. A forthcoming publication by Mathas
[
13
]
will confirm this, and we will use it freely.

(12)

v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\lambda}}g^{N}=0.

{\mathfrak{t}}^{\lambda}\sigma_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{N}}={\mathfrak{t}}^{N}
, then the Garnir relation is as follows.

v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\lambda}}\psi_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{N}}(2+\psi_{\sigma_{1}^{N}})=0.

2.5.
Stubborn Strings and Immobilization

The concept of stubborn strings was introduced by Lyle and Mathas in
[
12
]
, and they were developed further in
[
7
]
. They are a useful computational tool, especially for fully commutative string diagrams. We briefly recall the theory of stubborn strings as introduced in
[
7
]
. The setting of both papers is Specht modules of quiver Hecke algebras of type
A^{(1)}_{e-1}
, but the results hold for Specht modules of quiver Hecke algebras of type
C^{(1)}_{e}
as well.

Let
v\in S^{\lambda}
be a monomial. The set of
accessible nodes

A_{v}(s)
for a string
s
in
v
is defined in
[
7
]
. Intuitively, these are the nodes at the top of the diagram that
s
can reach by applying relations in
R_{n}^{\Lambda}
that either preserve crossings or cut them, but never create new crossings. Recall that for two nodes in a Young diagram
[\mu]
, we may write
N<M
if
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N)<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(M)
. In the string diagram, the nodes appear in this order at the top of the diagram.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
s
be a string in a monomial
v\in S^{\lambda}
.

(1)

We say
s
is
stubborn
if it reaches the smallest node in
A_{v}(s)
.

(2)

We say
s
is
co-stubborn
if it reaches the greatest node in
A_{v}(s)
.

We say that a string
s
of residue
i
is
immobile
if any relation that resolves an
(i,i)
-crossing of
s
yields zero. The simplest way for this to happen is if a string has a single accessible node, but it can happen in several ways. Immobile strings behave similarly to strings of a distinct residue: dots and strings may slide past their crossings without generating error terms. Specifically, the following relations hold for an immobile string
s
:

(13)

\hbox to33.91pt{\vbox to61.32pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\enskip\lower-11.47156pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}

In the remainder of this paper, we will make use of these relations for immobile strings.

3.
The Quiver Hecke Action on Hooks with a Short Arm or Leg

3.1.
y
-action

In this subsection we are concerned with describing the action of the nilpotent elements
y_{k}
(
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
) on a Specht module
S^{\lambda}
, where
\lambda\vdash n
is a hook with a short arm or leg. For convenience we will assume that the leg is short, but the arguments work equally well for
\lambda
with a short arm. It suffices to describe the action of
y_{k}
on the basis of
S^{\lambda}
given by
\{v_{\mathfrak{t}}\mid{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)\}
.

The
y
-action respects the residue sequence
\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})
, in the sense that
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\sum a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, where
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
and the sum is taken over all
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
such that
\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})=\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})
. For this reason, we will begin the study of the
y
-action on
S^{\lambda}
by examining the set
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})=\{{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)\mid\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})=\bm{i}\}
for an arbitrary
\bm{i}\in I^{n}
. In particular,
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
is non-empty whenever
e(\bm{i})S^{\lambda}\neq 0
.

Let
\bm{i}=(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
be a sequence of residues. A
substring
of
\bm{i}
is a subsequence formed by a contiguous block of indices, taking the form
(i_{j},i_{j+1},\dots,i_{m})
for some
1\leqslant j\leqslant m\leqslant n
. For instance,
(1,2,1,4)
is a substring of
(1,3,1,2,1,4,5)
, while
(1,3,5)
is not.

A weight
w\in Q^{+}
is said to be a
short weight
if
w=\wt(\bm{i})
, where

\bm{i}=(\overline{a\vphantom{b}},\overline{a+1\vphantom{b}},\dots,\overline{a+k})

where
a,k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
k<2e-1
. For instance,
\alpha_{0}+2\alpha_{1}
is a short weight, while
2\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{0}+2\alpha_{1}+\dots+2\alpha_{e-1}+\alpha_{e}
are not. Note also that a residue sequence
\bm{i}
like the above can alternatively be defined as a substring of the infinite residue sequence

(14)

\mathcal{I}\coloneq(\dots,1,0,1,\dots,e-1,e,e-1,\dots,1,0,1,\dots).

A weight
w
is said to be a
double short weight
if
w=2w^{\prime}
, where
w^{\prime}
is a short weight. A residue sequence
\bm{i}
is said to be a
(double) short residue sequence
if
\wt(\bm{i})
is a (double) short weight. A double short residue sequence
\bm{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{n})
is said to be
reduced
if there is no strict substring of the form
(i_{1},\dots,i_{k})
with
k<n
(that is, a strict initial segment starting at the exact same position) which is also a double short residue sequence. For instance, the sequence
\bm{i}=(1,0,1,1,0,1)
is a reduced double short residue sequence, while the sequence
\bm{i}=(1,1,2,2)
is not reduced because the subsequence
(1,1)
is also double short.

Next we list some easy facts about short weights.

Lemma 3.1
.

(1)

If
w
is a short weight, then the multiplicity of each
\alpha_{j}
is either
0,1
or
2
. Furthermore, if
j=0\text{ or }e
, then the multiplicity of
\alpha_{j}
is either
0
or
1
.

(2)

Let
w
be a short weight and
j\in I
. If there exists a substring
\bm{i}
of
\mathcal{I}
such that
i_{1}=j
and
\wt(\bm{i})=w
, then the sequence
\bm{i}
is unique. We write
\bm{i}_{j}(w)
for this residue sequence when it exists.

(3)

The weight
\wt(\bm{i})
of a substring
\bm{i}
of
\mathcal{I}
cannot be a double short weight.

(4)

There is a distance of at least
2e
between the starts of any two identical substrings of
\mathcal{I}
of length at least two.

We will use the above statements without explicit reference to them. The next lemma is crucial to the description of
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
w
be a short weight, and let
j\in I
be any residue. Let
\bm{i},\bm{j}
be substrings of
\mathcal{I}=(\dots,1,0,1,\dots,e-1,e,e-1,\dots)
such that
\wt(\bm{i})+\wt(\bm{j})=2w
and
i_{1}=j_{1}=j
. Then
\bm{i}_{j}(w)
exists, and furthermore
\bm{i}=\bm{j}=\bm{i}_{j}(w)
.

Proof.

We will show that
\wt(\bm{i})=\wt(\bm{j})=w
. Then it will follow by the previous lemma that
\bm{i}=\bm{j}=\bm{i}_{j}(w)
. There are several ways in which
\wt(\bm{i})
might differ from
\wt(\bm{j})
, and we will deal with each case separately.

(1)

First, suppose towards a contradiction that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{0}
in
\wt(\bm{i})
is
2
and the multiplicity of
\alpha_{0}
in
\wt(\bm{j})
is
0
.

(a)

If
\bm{i}=(j,j-1,\dots,0,1,2,\dots,2,1,0,\dots)
, then
\bm{j}=(j,j+1,\dots,e-1,e,\dots)
. We see that each residue between
1
and
e-1
appears at least three times in
\wt(\bm{i})+\wt(\bm{j})
, while
0
and
e
both appear twice, so it follows that
|\wt(\bm{i})+\wt(\bm{j})|=2w\geqslant 4e
, so that
w
is not a short weight.

(b)

If
\bm{i}=(j,j+1,\dots,e-1,e,e-1,\dots,0,1,2,\dots,2,1,0,\dots)
, then
\bm{j}=(j,j-1,\dots,2,1)
, and a similar argument shows that
|\wt(\bm{i})+\wt(\bm{j})|\geqslant 4e
, so that
w
is not a short weight.

It follows that, whenever the multiplicity of
\alpha_{0}
in
w
is
1
, we must have that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{0}
in both
\wt(\bm{i})
and
\wt(\bm{j})
is
1
. The analogous statement holds for the residue
e
.

(2)

Next we let
k\in I\setminus\{0,e\}
and suppose that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
\wt(\bm{i})
is
2
and the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
\wt(\bm{j})
is
0
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\bm{i}=(j,\dots,k,k+1,\dots,e-1,e,e-1,\dots,k+1,k,\dots)
. From the above, we know that
e
may not appear twice inside
\bm{i}
, so that
e
must appear once inside
\bm{j}
. We must have
j\leqslant k
, since otherwise
\bm{i}
would include more than two
k
’s. Therefore,
\bm{j}
starts with
j\leqslant k
, contains no
k
’s, and one
e
. This is a contradiction, and therefore whenever the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
w
is
1
, we must have that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in both
\wt(\bm{i})
and
\wt(\bm{j})
is
1
.

(3)

Next we let
k\in I\setminus\{0,e\}
and suppose that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
\wt(\bm{i})
is
4
and the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
\wt(\bm{j})
is
0
. It is clear that
\bm{i}
contains one of either
0
or
e
between each pair of
k
’s. Since
\bm{i}
contains four
k
’s, it contains at least two of either
0
or
e
. This contradicts
1
.

(4)

Finally, we let
k\in I\setminus\{0,e\}
and suppose that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
\wt(\bm{i})
is
3
and the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
\wt(\bm{j})
is
1
. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
\bm{i}=(j,j+1,\dots)
. We know that
\bm{i}
has one of
0
or
e
between each pair of
k
’s. Due to
1
, we must have

\bm{i}=(j,j+1,\dots,k,\dots,e,\dots,k,\dots,0,\dots,k,\dots).

Note that
\bm{i}
includes at least three
j
’s. Therefore,
\bm{j}
must contain exactly one of each
0
,
k
and
e
. Moreover,
\bm{j}
starts with
j
, but it may contain at most one
j
. It is easy to see that this is absurd, and therefore whenever the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in
w
is
2
, we must have that the multiplicity of
\alpha_{k}
in both
\wt(\bm{i})
and
\wt(\bm{j})
is
2
, completing the proof. ∎

Let
\bm{i}\in I^{n}
be a residue sequence such that
e(\bm{i})S^{\lambda}\neq 0
. For any tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
, it is clear that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(1)=(1,1)
. Suppose we know
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
for some
m\geqslant 1
and we wish to determine
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)
. The unfilled nodes in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
each form a contiguous strip starting
from the current boundary of
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
. We denote the sequences of
residues of these unfilled nodes, ordered by their distance from the corner
node
(1,1)
, as:

\bm{i}^{\text{arm}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m})=(i^{\text{arm}}_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}),i^{\text{arm}}_{2}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}),\dots)\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{i}^{\text{leg}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m})=(i^{\text{leg}}_{1}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}),i^{\text{leg}}_{2}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}),\dots).

We omit the argument
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
when it is clear from context. Next we are interested in placing the number
m+1\leqslant n
in
{\mathfrak{t}}
. The residue
i_{m+1}
must equal at least one of
i^{\text{arm}}_{1}
or
i^{\text{leg}}_{1}
, and if
i_{m+1}\neq i^{\text{arm}}_{1}
, then
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. Similarly, if
i_{m+1}\neq i^{\text{leg}}_{1}
, then
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
. Suppose instead that
i_{m+1}=i^{\text{arm}}_{1}=i^{\text{leg}}_{1}
. We split our investigation into two possibilities:

(1)

Suppose that there exists a substring
\bm{i}^{\prime}
of
\bm{i}
beginning at
i^{\prime}_{1}=i_{m+1}
such that
\wt(\bm{i}^{\prime})=2w
is a double short weight, and let
l^{\prime}\coloneq\ell(\bm{i}^{\prime})
. Then, according to
3.2
, the numbers
m+1,\dots,m+l^{\prime}
can be divided into two halves
A
and
B
of equal size so that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(a)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
if
a\in A
, and
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(b)\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
if
b\in B
. Furthermore,
\sum_{a\in A}\alpha_{\res({\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(a))}=\sum_{b\in B}\alpha_{\res({\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(b))}=w
. If the numbers in
A
and
B
are exchanged in
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, the resulting tableau is also standard.

(2)

Suppose, to the contrary, that no substring
\bm{i}^{\prime}
of
\bm{i}
beginning at
i^{\prime}_{1}=i_{m+1}
exists such that
\wt(\bm{i}^{\prime})=2w
is a double short weight, and in particular
i_{m+2}\neq i_{m+1}
. We claim that in this case, the node
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)
is fully determined by the residue sequence
\bm{i}
.

(a)

Suppose that
i_{m+2}\neq i^{\text{arm}}_{2}
. Then it is clear that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. Similarly, if
i_{m+2}\neq i^{\text{leg}}_{2}
, then
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
.

(b)

Suppose, to the contrary, that
i_{m+2}=i^{\text{arm}}_{2}=i^{\text{leg}}_{2}
.

(i)

Suppose that
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
share the same number of unfilled nodes. Then the sequence
\bm{i}^{\prime}=(i_{m+1},i_{m+2},\dots,i_{n})
has
\wt(\bm{i}^{\prime})=2w
, where
w
is the short weight corresponding to the unfilled nodes in both the arm and leg. (Recall that in this section
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
is assumed to be short.)

(ii)

Suppose that
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
has more unfilled nodes than
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
in
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
and
i_{m+2}=i^{\text{arm}}_{2}=i^{\text{leg}}_{2}
. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. Let
m^{\prime}>m
be an integer such that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m^{\prime}}\setminus{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow m}
has the same number of filled nodes in the arm as in the leg. Note that
m^{\prime}>m
must exist, since
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
has more unfilled nodes than
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. Then the sequence
\bm{i}^{\prime}=(i_{m+1},\dots,i_{m^{\prime}})
has a double short weight
\wt(\bm{i}^{\prime})=2w
(recall that
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
is short), which contradicts our assumption at the start of (2). Therefore,
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(m+1)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
. A similar conclusion holds if
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
has more unfilled nodes than
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
.

We conclude that the construction of any
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
by placing the natural numbers into the Young diagram proceeds deterministically except at specific points where a binary choice between the arm and the leg arises.

Definition 3.3
.

The set of
designated sequences

DS(\bm{i})=\{\bm{s}^{(1)},\dots,\bm{s}^{(d)}\}
is the set containing all contiguous integer intervals
\bm{s}=[u,v]\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
satisfying the following two conditions:

(1)

The subword of residues corresponding to the interval,
\bm{i}_{\bm{s}}=(i_{u},i_{u+1},\dots,i_{v})
, is a reduced double short residue sequence.

(2)

For any
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
, consider the subtableau
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow u-1}
. The next available addable nodes in both
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
exist, and their residues are both equal to
i_{u}
(i.e.,
i_{1}^{\text{arm}}=i_{1}^{\text{leg}}=i_{u}
).

For each
r\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, we denote the indices of the
r
-th interval by
\bm{s}^{(r)}=(s^{(r)}_{1},s^{(r)}_{2},\dots,\allowbreak s^{(r)}_{|\bm{s}^{(r)}|})
and its corresponding residue sequence by
\bm{i}^{(r)}\coloneq\bm{i}_{\bm{s}^{(r)}}
. Since a reduced double short residue sequence is uniquely determined by its starting position, the intervals in
DS(\bm{i})
have distinct starting points, and we may unambiguously order them according to their starting indices so that
{s}^{(1)}_{1}<{s}^{(2)}_{1}<\dots<{s}^{(d)}_{1}
.

It is important to verify that the set
DS(\bm{i})
is well-defined, that is, that Condition (2) does not depend on the choice of
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
. Since designated sequences can be nested (that is, an interval
\bm{s}^{(2)}
may be strictly contained within another interval
\bm{s}^{(1)}
), the precise shape of the subtableau
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow(u_{2}-1)}
for
u_{2}={s}^{(2)}_{1}
may depend on whether the initial elements of the larger sequence
\bm{s}^{(1)}
were assigned to
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
or
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. In order to deal with this issue, first we describe the subset structure of
DS(\bm{i})
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
r_{1},r_{2}\in\{1,\dots,d\}
with
r_{1}<r_{2}
. Suppose that
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}\cap\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\neq\varnothing
. Then
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\subset\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
,
|\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}|=2
and the repeated residue in
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}
is not
0
or
e
.

Proof.

Let
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}=[u_{1},v_{1}]
and
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}=[u_{2},v_{2}]
. Since
r_{1}<r_{2}
and the designated sequences are ordered by their starting index, we have
u_{1}<u_{2}
. The assumption
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}\cap\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\neq\varnothing
implies
u_{2}\leqslant v_{1}
. We first show that
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\subset\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
, which is equivalent to showing
v_{2}\leqslant v_{1}
. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\not\subset\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
. Let us first check that, under our supposition, both
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
and
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}
have length larger than 2. Indeed, suppose that
|\bm{s}^{(1)}|=2
. Then, since we have both
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\not\subset\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
and
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}\cap\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\neq\varnothing
, it follows that
u_{2}=u_{1}+1=v_{1}
, which indicates that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow u_{1}}
has two addable nodes with residue
i_{v_{1}}=i_{u_{1}}
, after placing
u_{1}
in a node with that residue. This is impossible, so it follows that
|\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}|>2
. A similar argument shows that
|\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}|>2
.

Since
|\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}|>2
, the addable sequences
\bm{i}^{\text{arm}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow(u_{1}-1)})
and
\bm{i}^{\text{leg}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow(u_{1}-1)})
coincide until one of them terminates, since their first two residues coincide. The same can be said about
\bm{i}^{\text{arm}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow(u_{2}-1)})
and
\bm{i}^{\text{leg}}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow(u_{2}-1)})
. The amounts of nodes in the arm and leg covered by numbers in
\bm{s^{(r_{1})}}
are equal, and a similar result holds for
\bm{s^{(r_{2})}}
. Consequently, the amounts of nodes covered by the numbers
[u_{1},u_{2}-1]
in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
are equal. Therefore, the sequence
\bm{s}^{*}=[u_{1},u_{2}-1]
is itself a double short sequence. This contradicts the assumption that the designated sequence
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
must be reduced, according to the definition of
DS(\bm{i})
. This completes the proof that
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}\subset\bm{s}^{r_{1}}
.

The sequence
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}
must fill subsegments of equal length within the segments filled by
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
. Let
d_{\text{arm}}
and
d_{\text{leg}}
be the respective number of nodes placed in the arm and in the leg by
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
prior to the start of
\bm{s}^{(r_{2})}
. We must have
d_{\text{arm}}\neq d_{\text{leg}}
, as otherwise the subsequence
\bm{s}^{*}
would be a double short sequence, contradicting the reducedness of
\bm{s}^{(r_{1})}
.

Define
\bm{i}_{\text{arm}}^{(r)}
to be the residue sequence in the arm of
{\mathfrak{t}}
filled by the numbers in
\bm{s}^{r}
, and define
\bm{i}_{\text{leg}}^{(r)}
analogously. The sequence
\bm{i}_{\text{arm}}^{(r_{2})}
appears at two distinct starting positions (
d_{\text{arm}}+1
and
d_{\text{leg}}+1
) inside
\bm{i}_{\text{arm}}^{(r_{1})}
. But the starts of two identical substrings of
\mathcal{I}
of length at least two are separated by at least
2e
residues, and of course the residues
0
and
e
are also placed at intervals of
2e
. Since
\bm{i}_{\text{arm}}^{(r_{1})}
is a short residue sequence, its total length is strictly less than
2e
. It follows that
\bm{i}^{(r_{2})}_{\text{arm}}
cannot have length 2 or greater, and thus must contain a single residue, which cannot be
0
or
e
. This completes the proof.
∎

Let us return to our goal of checking that Condition 2 of the definition of
DS(\bm{i})
is well-defined. Suppose we have
\bm{s}^{(2)}\subset\bm{s}^{(1)}
. Since
|\bm{s}^{(1)}|>2
, then the addable residue sequences in the arm and leg will be symmetric at
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow{(u_{1}-1)}}
, where
u_{1}={s}^{(1)}_{1}
. After placing
u_{1}
, the numbers
u_{1}+1,\dots,u_{2}-1
are each forced into either
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
or
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
, and the next available nodes in both
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
and
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
at step
u_{2}
will share the same residue
i_{u_{2}}
. We then make an independent choice (
A
or
L
) for the elements of the short sequence
\bm{s}^{(2)}
. Since
\bm{s}^{(2)}
consumes exactly one node in the arm and one in the leg, the set of filled nodes does not depend on this choice. By the time the numbers in the larger sequence
\bm{s}^{(1)}
have all been placed in
{\mathfrak{t}}
, the resulting Young diagram shape is independent of these intermediate choices, as a sequence of nodes with weight
w(\bm{i})
has been filled in both the arm and leg of
\lambda
. This guarantees that the process is deterministic outside of these specific binary decisions, and that an earlier
(A,L)
-choice does not affect the structure of
DS(\bm{i})
.

We partition the index set
\{1,\dots,d\}
into two disjoint subsets
W_{2}
and
W_{4}
, where
r\in W_{4}
if
\bm{i}^{(r)}\in\{(0,0),(e,e)\}
, and
r\in W_{2}
otherwise.

The next proposition synthesizes our findings and parameterizes the set
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
via these independent binary choices.

Proposition 3.5
.

Suppose that
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})\neq\varnothing
. Let
\{A,L\}^{d}
be the set of words of length
d
in the alphabet
\{A,L\}
. Then there is a bijection
\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}:\{A,L\}^{d}\rightarrow\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
uniquely determined by the property that for any
r\in\{1,\dots,d\}
, the node
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(s^{(r)}_{1})
belongs to
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
if and only if the
r
-th letter of the word is
A
. Furthermore, if
{\mathfrak{t}}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})
, its degree is given by

\deg({\mathfrak{t}})=\deg(\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(A,\dots,A))-2\#\{r\in W_{2}\mid l_{r}=L\}-4\#\{r\in W_{4}\mid l_{r}=L\}.

We say that
(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})
is the
binary word
of
{\mathfrak{t}}
.

Proof.

The existence of the bijection
\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}
follows from the preceding discussion. We must prove the statement about the degrees. By an inductive argument, it is enough to determine
\deg({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})-\deg({\mathfrak{t}})
in the case of two tableaux
{\mathfrak{t}},{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
where

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{t}}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{r-1},A,l_{r+1},\dots,l_{d}),

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{r-1},L,l_{r+1},\dots,l_{d}).

Suppose that
\bm{i}^{(r)}
has length
2q
. We restrict our attention to the row- and column-strips in
\lambda
which house the numbers in
\bm{s}^{(r)}=\{s^{(r)}_{1},\dots,s^{(r)}_{2q}\}
. If we focus our attention on the relevant strings only, then the computation in
3.10
shows that
\deg(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{L}})-\deg(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{A}})=2
whenever
r\in W_{2}
, and the case
r\in W_{4}
is trivial.
∎

Corollary 3.6
.

There exists a unique tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i})\coloneq\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(A,\dots,A)\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
of maximal degree inside
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
.

Example 3.7
.

Suppose
e=2
and
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}
. Let
\lambda=(4,1^{3})\vdash 7
and fix the residue sequence
\bm{i}=(0,1,2,1,1,2,1)
.

By mapping these residues onto the nodes of the hook
\lambda
, we find two points where an identical residue can be placed in either the arm or the leg, leading to two designated sequences in
DS(\bm{i})
:

•

\bm{s}^{(1)}=(2,3,4,5,6,7)
with
\bm{i}^{(1)}=(1,2,1,1,2,1)
.

•

\bm{s}^{(2)}=(4,5)
with
\bm{i}^{(2)}=(1,1)
.

Note that the double short residue sequence
\bm{s}^{*}=(3,4,5,6)
is not a designated sequence in
DS(\bm{i})
as it does not satisfy condition 2: at the time of placing the number
3
, the tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow 2}
can only be one of

and in either case there is only one available node with the correct residue.

Thus,
d=2
. Since neither sequence is
(0,0)
or
(e,e)
, both belong to
W_{2}
. Note that
\bm{s}^{(2)}\subset\bm{s}^{(1)}
.
The bijection
\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}
gives four standard tableaux in
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
corresponding to the words in
\{A,L\}^{2}
:

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Now that we understand the set
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
, we are ready to explore the effect of the
y
-action on a basis vector
v_{\mathfrak{t}}\in S^{\lambda}
with
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
. That is, we are interested in finding the coefficients
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{Z}
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})}a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
.

Let us first compare the diagram
v_{\mathfrak{t}}=v_{\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{r-1},A,l_{r+1},\dots,l_{d})}
to the diagram
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\coloneq v_{\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{r-1}^{\prime},L,l_{r+1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{d}^{\prime})}
. Recall that for any fully commutative standard monomial, every string in the diagram is either stubborn or co-stubborn. In our specific configuration, a string routed to the arm is stubborn, while a string routed to the leg is co-stubborn. In the diagram for
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}}
, the choice
A
dictates that the string
s^{(r)}_{1}
is routed to the arm. Because
s^{(r)}_{1}
is stubborn, it cannot reach larger nodes, meaning it is geometrically impossible for it to reach any node located in the leg in any non-zero resolution of crossings. It follows directly that an
A
choice cannot transform into an
L
choice through the
y
-action. Consequently, if
l_{r}=A
for
{\mathfrak{t}}
, then
l_{r}=A
for any
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
such that
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\neq 0
in the decomposition of
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}}y_{k}
. Together with the degree considerations from
section

3.1
, we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
{\mathfrak{t}}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
.

(1)

If
\deg(y_{k})=2
, then
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})}a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, with
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\neq 0
only if
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{d}^{\prime})
, with
l_{s}=l_{s}^{\prime}
for
s\in\{1,\dots,r-1,r+1,\dots,d\}
, while
l_{r}=L
,
l_{r}^{\prime}=A
,
k\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
and
r\in W_{2}
.

(2)

If
\deg(y_{k})=4
, then
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, with
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{d}^{\prime})
, with
l_{s}=l_{s}^{\prime}
for all
s\in\{1,\dots,r-1,r+1,\dots,d\}
, while
l_{r}=L
,
l_{r}^{\prime}=A
,
k\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
and
r\in W_{4}
.

Proof.

Indeed, we know that
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, where
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\neq 0
only if

(1)

{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
,

(2)

\deg({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})-\deg({\mathfrak{t}})=\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))
and

(3)

The node
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})^{-1}(k)
is accessible to string
k
in the diagram
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
. That is,
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})^{-1}(k)\in A_{v_{\mathfrak{t}}}(k)
.

Finally, according to
[
7
, Lemma 3.6]
, after cutting a crossing of string
k
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, the original node
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(k)
that string
k
reached in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
is no longer accessible to
k
, so that one of the binary choices involving string
k
must change.
The case where
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=2
now immediately follows.

Moreover, a residue
0
or
e
may be at most inside one reduced residue sequence of double short weight, as shown in
3.4
. It follows that there is at most one summand in the basis decomposition of
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}
whenever
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=4
. ∎

Incidentally, the usual tableau dominance order relates nicely to the function
\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}
. To be precise, we have

{\mathfrak{t}}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})\vartriangleright\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{d}^{\prime})={\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}

if and only if
(l_{r},l_{r}^{\prime})\neq(L,A)
for all
r\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. In particular,
{\mathfrak{t}}\vartriangleright{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
implies
\deg({\mathfrak{t}})>\deg({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})
.

The above lemma provides us with an accurate idea of the terms that vanish under the
y
-action. However, to find the value of
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
in the remaining cases we will compute it via the diagram calculus of quiver Hecke algebras.

Let
s\in\{1,\dots,n\}
be such that
s\notin\bm{s}^{(r)}
for any
r
such that
k\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
. Then
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}=0
whenever
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(s)\neq({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})^{-1}(s)
, in concordance with
3.8
. Therefore all such strings are immobile with respect to the action of
y_{k}
. Since they are immobile strings in a diagram
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
with a fully commutative underlying permutation
\sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}
, we may fairly stop drawing them when computing
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}
. In our computations we will only draw the strings labelled by an integer in
\bm{s}^{(r)}
. Recall that the residues sequence attached to the numbers in
\bm{s}^{(r)}=\{s^{(r)}_{1},\dots,s^{(r)}_{2m}\}
is
i_{1}^{(r)},\dots,i_{2m}^{(r)}
. We will use the simple notation
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
for the (equal) short residue sequences filled in the arm and leg of
\lambda
by
\bm{s}^{(r)}
. These appear in a row-strip in the arm and in a column-strip in the leg of
\lambda
.

Next we give a description of the diagram for the strings
\bm{s}^{(r)}
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, assuming that
l_{r}=L
for
{\mathfrak{t}}
. This condition ensures that
s_{1}^{(r)}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
in
{\mathfrak{t}}
. Moreover, we assume that
\bm{i}^{(r)}
is reduced, which requires that
s_{2}^{(r)}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. By the same reasoning,
s^{(r)}_{3}
and
s^{(r)}_{4}
may not both be in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
. In general, for any
k<\frac{m}{2}
, at least
k+1
out of the first
2k
strings must reach
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
.

Next we give a description of the diagram for
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
. For graphical clarity, we have used the notation
i_{m}^{-j}\coloneq i_{m-j}
. The column-strip satisfies the same relations as the row-strip, the Garnir relations in the column-strip playing the role of the Specht relations in the row-strip. For this reason, we have chosen to draw the column-strip as a row-strip.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{r-1},L,l_{r+1},\dots,l_{d})
be the tableau described above, and draw only the strings in
\bm{s}^{(r)}
. Then

(15)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}=\hbox to198.25pt{\vbox to115.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.29863pt\lower-41.97578pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces;
;
\par{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{}}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 23.62 -47.24 L 23.62 -5.53 C 23.62 -2.48 25.68 1.37 28.23 3.07 L 141.73 78.74}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{98.46274pt}{62.23766pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{{}}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 -47.24 L 39.37 -5.53 C 39.37 -2.48 41.43 1.37 43.97 3.07 L 157.48 78.74}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}},

where the permutation
\sigma
is restricted in the following two ways:

(1)

\sigma
may not undo any crossings in the diagram above it.

(2)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}
is the basis element for a standard tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}
.

We denote the upper part of Diagram
15
by
v_{L}
, so that
v_{\mathfrak{t}}=v_{L}\psi_{\sigma}
.

Proof.

We work by induction on
m
. The result is clear for
m=2
. Write
N_{1},\dots,N_{m}
for the nodes in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
reached by strings in
\bm{s}^{(r)}
, and write
N_{1}^{\prime},\dots,N_{m}^{\prime}
for the corresponding nodes in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. Next, ignore the strings reaching nodes
N_{m},N_{m}^{\prime}
. In this way we obtain a diagram with
2m-2
strings having a reduced sequence with double short weight. By the inductive hypothesis, this diagram looks as follows, for some permutation
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfying the appropriate conditions.

(16)

i_{1}
i_{2}
i_{1}
i_{3}
i_{2}
i_{4}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
i_{m}^{-3}
i_{m}^{-1}
i_{m}^{-2}
i_{m}^{-1}
\psi_{\sigma^{\prime}}

Next we consider the ways in which the strings reaching nodes
N_{m},N_{m}^{\prime}
may appear in the diagram. Standardness requires that node
N_{m}
is filled with a number higher than
{\mathfrak{t}}(N_{m-1})
. Moreover, the nodes
\{N_{m},N_{m}^{\prime}\}
may not be filled with numbers
\{s^{(r)}_{2m-1},s^{(r)}_{2m}\}
, since
\bm{s}^{(r)}
must be reduced. Together, these conditions imply that
{\mathfrak{t}}(N_{m})=s^{(r)}_{2m}
, while the string labeled by
{\mathfrak{t}}(N_{m}^{\prime})
crosses at least the strings
s^{(r)}_{2m-1},s^{(r)}_{2m}
. It may then go on to cross further strings. Therefore, the diagram for
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
looks as follows.

(17)

\hbox to198.25pt{\vbox to138.45pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.29863pt\lower-64.73766pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces;

where the permutation
\tau
exchanges only the string labeled by
{\mathfrak{t}}(N_{m}^{\prime})
with other strings. Renaming
\psi_{\sigma}=\psi_{\sigma^{\prime}}\psi_{\tau}=\psi_{\sigma^{\prime}\tau}
, we easily see that
\sigma
can be any permutation satisfying conditions
1
and
2
.
∎

Next we wish to compute
v_{L}y_{k}
, which will naturally be useful in the course of computing
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=v_{L}\psi_{\sigma}y_{k}
. In the diagram we will use an operator
\phi_{ab}
which we define to be a crossing between two strings unless
i_{a}=i_{b}
, in which case
\phi_{ab}=1
. Furthermore, we will use the compact notation
\phi_{-ab}\coloneq\phi_{m-a,m-b}
to avoid visual clutter.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
m\geqslant 2
,
k\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
with
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=2
, and let
v_{L}\in S^{\lambda}
be as in
3.9
. Then we have

v_{L}y_{k}=\pm\hbox to198.25pt{\vbox to115.69pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 7.29863pt\lower-41.97578pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces;

. But breaking a crossing above the first crossing immediately gives zero due to a Specht relation, so that Relation

gives simply

past the crossings of
s^{(r)}_{2}
until it reaches the top of the diagram, where the diagram evaluates to zero due to a Specht relation. Again, the only non-zero term corresponds to the summand where the lowest crossing of
s^{(r)}_{2}
is cut, where Relation
5
gives

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}Y\eqcolon\pm\sum_{Y}v_{A}^{\prime}Y

where the sum is over all collections
Y
of dots having one dot per pair of strings
s_{a},s_{a}^{\prime}\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
which, in
v_{L}
, reach nodes
N_{a},N_{a}^{\prime}
to the right of nodes
N_{a-1},N_{a-1}^{\prime}
of residue
0
or
e
. That is, in
v_{L}
, string
s_{a}
reaches
N_{a}\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
, while
s_{a}^{\prime}
reaches
N_{a}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. However, the string
s_{a}^{\prime}
does not meet any other strings of its residue in
v_{A}^{\prime}
, so that
v_{A}^{\prime}y_{s_{a}^{\prime}}=0
. It follows that the only non-zero summand in Diagram
21
which does not vanish is given by the
Y
where all the
s_{a}
have a dot and none of the
s_{a}^{\prime}
have a dot. These dots break all the crossings between strings of the same residue, and we reach the diagram in the lemma statement.
∎

Next we wish to compute
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}
in more generality. Let
\sigma
be an arbitrary permutation that satisfies conditions
1
and
2
above. Suppose first that
k\in\bm{s}^{(r+1)}\subset\bm{s}^{(r)}
and
l_{r+1}=L
in
{\mathfrak{t}}
. According to
3.4
,
\bm{i}^{(r+1)}=(i,i)
, so that string
k
is adjacent to and crosses a string with the same residue in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Suppose for instance that
i_{k+1}=i_{k}
, so that
v_{\mathfrak{t}}=v^{\prime}\psi_{k}
. In that case,

(22)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=v^{\prime}\psi_{k}y_{k}=(v^{\prime}y_{k+1})\psi_{k}-v^{\prime},

according to
eq.

6
. It follows that to understand the
y
-action on
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, it is enough to understand the action on
v^{\prime}
. In the sequel we make the further assumption that in
\psi_{\sigma}
, two adjacent strings of the same residue do not cross each other.

Lemma 3.11
.

Let
v_{\mathfrak{t}}=v_{L}\psi_{\sigma}
, where
\sigma
is a permutation satisfying conditions
1
,
2
, and suppose that adjacent strings of equal residue do not cross each other in
\psi_{\sigma}
. Suppose also that
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=2
. Then

v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\pm v_{A}\psi_{\sigma}.

Proof.

The following is a sketch of the strings involved in the argument as they appear in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
.

(23)

t^{\prime}
i_{k}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
k^{\prime}
i_{k}
k
i_{k}
t
i_{k}
s^{\prime}
i_{k}^{-1}
s
i_{k}^{-1}
u
i_{k}^{+1}

We are tasked with showing that
v_{L}\psi_{\sigma}y_{k}=v_{L}y_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}\psi_{\sigma}
. The lemma will easily follow, as

(24)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=v_{L}\psi_{\sigma}y_{k}=v_{L}y_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}\psi_{\sigma}=\pm v_{A}\psi_{\sigma}.

The reduced expression for
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
is fully commutative, so that the string labelled by
k
is either stubborn or co-stubborn. We suppose without loss of generality that it is stubborn. As the dot
y_{k}
slides past the crossings of string
k
, it may encounter a crossing with a co-stubborn string of the same residue before reaching
v_{L}
. We label this string
k^{\prime}
. The dot slide leaves a summand
\pm v^{*}_{1}
where the crossing between strings
k
and
k^{\prime}
is cut, according to
eq.

6
. To prove the lemma it suffices to show that
v^{*}_{1}=0
.

First, notice that for strings
k
and
k^{\prime}
to cross below
v_{L}
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, residue
i_{k}
must appear four times inside
\bm{i}^{(r)}
, string
k
must reach the smallest node of its residue and
k^{\prime}
must reach the largest node of its residue. Moreover, strings
k,k^{\prime}
may not be adjacent, according to the condition imposed on
\psi_{\sigma}
in the statement of the lemma. Consequently, there is at least one string between
k^{\prime}
and
k
, and at least one of the following holds for the tableau corresponding to
v_{1}^{*}
.

(1)

The number in the node immediately to the left of
{\mathfrak{t}}_{{v^{*}_{1}}}^{-1}(k)
is smaller than
k
, or

(2)

The number in the node immediately to the right of
{\mathfrak{t}}_{{v^{*}_{1}}}^{-1}(k^{\prime})
is larger than
k^{\prime}
.

We suppose that the former condition holds without loss of generality, and we let the number in question be
s^{\prime}
. It has residue
i_{k-1}
. (Recall that we are using the notation
i_{1},\dots,i_{m}
for the short residue sequence in the arm and leg covered by
\bm{s}^{(r)}
.)

String
k
can slide through all the crossings of string
s^{\prime}
, leaving summands where one of these crossings is cut. If string
k
slides past all the crossings of string
s^{\prime}
without cutting any, the diagram vanishes due to
eq.

10
. In fact, exactly one of the crossings of
s^{\prime}
which string
k
slides past features a co-stubborn string
s
sharing the residue
i_{k-1}=\res(s^{\prime})
. This is because, if
s^{\prime}
reaches
N\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, then there is a corresponding string
s
reaching the mirrored node
N^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
in
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
, and standardness requires
s>k
. If there were another string
s^{\prime\prime}
with residue
i_{k-1}
reaching a node in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
, we would have
s^{\prime\prime}<k
, as this string must reach a node lower than
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(k)
.

We call
v^{*}_{2}
the diagram obtained from
v^{*}_{1}
by cutting the crossing between the co-stubborn string
s
and the stubborn string
s^{\prime}
via the slide of string
k
past this crossing, according to
eq.

5
. The
(s,s^{\prime})
-crossing in
v^{*}_{1}
is part of the middle horizontal of
(i,i)
-crossings in the upper part of the diagram
v^{*}_{1}
described by
v_{L}
, so that, in diagram
v^{*}_{2}
, string
s^{\prime}
can slide past the crossings of a stubborn string
t
of residue
i_{k}
, again vanishing at the top of the diagram due to
eq.

10
and leaving only a diagram
v^{*}_{3}
where the crossing between string
t
and a co-stubborn string
t^{\prime}
of residue
i_{k}
is cut.

Finally, in diagram
v^{*}_{3}
, string
t^{\prime}
can slide past the crossings of a string
u
of residue
i_{k+1}
without meeting any
(i_{k+1},i_{k+1})
-crossings, so that
v^{*}_{3}=0
due to
eq.

10
, and therefore
v^{*}_{1}=0
, which completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Now we have reached the final problem in this section: we need to compute
v_{A}\psi_{\sigma}
. Recall that we’re currently making the assumption that adjacent strings of equal residue do not cross.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{r-1},A,l_{r+1},\dots,l_{d})
and let
v_{A}
be as in
3.10
. Then

v_{A}\psi_{\sigma}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}.

Proof.

According to
3.8
and
3.11
,
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\pm v_{A}\psi_{\sigma}=a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
. We must show that
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}=\pm 1
. Let
\sigma^{*}
be a permutation so that
v_{L}\psi_{\sigma^{*}}
exchanges all stubborn strings with all co-stubborn strings. It is clear that
\sigma^{*}
is the longest (in Bruhat order) permutation satisfying conditions
1
and
2
, so that for any
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfying those two conditions, we have
\psi_{\sigma^{\prime}}\psi_{\tau_{\sigma^{\prime}}}=\psi_{\sigma^{*}}
for some permutation
\tau_{\sigma^{\prime}}
. It is enough to show that
v_{A}\psi_{\sigma^{*}}
gives a diagram
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}}
in the Specht module basis with coefficient
\pm 1
. Indeed, in that case,

v_{A}\psi_{\sigma^{*}}=v_{A}\psi_{\sigma}\psi_{\tau_{\sigma}}=a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}\psi_{\tau_{\sigma}}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}},

so it follows that
a_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}=\pm 1
.

We proceed to the computation of
v_{A}\psi_{\sigma^{*}}
. For the purposes of induction, we will perform a slightly more general computation in resolving the computation in
eq.

25
under the following conditions for
v
, which are easily seen to hold for the center strings of diagram
v_{A}
.

(1)

The residue sequences
\bm{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{m})
and
\bm{j}=(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})
are both short residue sequences.

(2)

The parity of
i_{1}
and
j_{1}
coincides, so that:

(a)

i_{a}=j_{b}
only if
a-b
is even.

(b)

i_{a}
is adjacent to
j_{b}
only if
a-b
is odd.

(3)

The following configurations are
not
allowed:

(a)

(i_{a},i_{a+1})=(j_{b},j_{b+1})
for any
b\leqslant a
.

(b)

(i_{a},i_{a+1},j_{b},j_{b+1})
is a substring of
\mathcal{I}
, for any
b\leqslant a
.

(4)

A string of residue
i=0
or
e
only crosses strings of residues
j{\text{ $\not$--- }}i
in
v
.

(25)

{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

For the purposes of this diagram we define
\phi_{ab}
in close analogy with
3.10
, so that
\phi_{ab}
represents a crossing if and only if
i_{a}\neq j_{b}
. We have used the notation
\phi_{-ab}\coloneq\phi_{m-a,m-b}
for graphical considerations. Next we perform an induction on
m
to show that
eq.

25
holds. The statement can be easily checked for
m=1,2
.

Next suppose that
i_{1}=j_{1}
. Then condition (3.a) implies that
i_{2}{\text{ $\not$--- }}j_{2}
, so that near the center-left of the diagram we have

(26)

Here we have written
\phi_{aa}^{+1}\coloneq\phi_{a+1,a+1}
.
Next suppose that
i_{a}=j_{a}
, with
a\notin\{1,m\}
. Then condition (3.a) implies that
i_{a+1}{\text{ $\not$--- }}j_{a+1}
and
i_{a-1}{\text{ $\not$--- }}j_{a-1}
, which implies that
i_{a+1}=j_{a-1}
. In this case, near the center of the diagram we find

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Resolving this diagram corresponds exactly to solving a version of
eq.

25
for
m^{\prime}=m-2
, and it is easy to see that the conditions above that equation also hold for the new diagram, so that, by induction,
eq.

25
holds. This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The results of this section come together nicely to give a complete description (up to sign) of the type C
y
-action on a Specht module with a short leg. This is the main theorem of this section.

Theorem 3.13
.

Let
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
{\mathfrak{t}}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
.

(1)

If
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=2
, then
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\sum_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})}\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, where the sum is taken over
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
such that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{d}^{\prime})
, with
l_{s}=l_{s}^{\prime}
for
s\in\{1,\dots,r-1,r+1,\dots,d\}
, while
l_{r}=L
,
l_{r}^{\prime}=A
,
k\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
and
r\in W_{2}
.

(2)

If
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=4
, then
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{k}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, with
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1}^{\prime},\dots,l_{d}^{\prime})
, with
l_{s}=l_{s}^{\prime}
for all
s\in\{1,\dots,r-1,r+1,\dots,d\}
, while
l_{r}=L
,
l_{r}^{\prime}=A
,
k\in\bm{s}^{(r)}
and
r\in W_{4}
.

Example 3.14
.

We continue
section

3.1
. Let us evaluate the
y
-action on the basis vector
v_{\phi(L,L)}
according to
section

3.1
:

•

For
k\in\{2,7\}
, we have
k\in\bm{s}^{(1)}
but
k\notin\bm{s}^{(2)}
. The action flips
l_{1}
from
L
to
A
, yielding exactly one term:

v_{\phi(L,L)}y_{2}=v_{\phi(L,L)}y_{7}=\pm v_{\phi(A,L)}.

•

For
k\in\{4,5\}
, we have
k\in\bm{s}^{(1)}\cap\bm{s}^{(2)}
. Summing over
r\in\{1,2\}
flips
l_{1}\to A
and
l_{2}\to A
, yielding a linear combination of two terms:

v_{\phi(L,L)}y_{4}=\pm v_{\phi(A,L)}\pm v_{\phi(L,A)},

v_{\phi(L,L)}y_{5}=\pm v_{\phi(A,L)}\pm v_{\phi(L,A)}.

We make no claims regarding the signs.

•

For
k\in\{3,6\}
, we have
\deg(y_{k}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=4
and no subsequences in
W_{4}
, so that
v_{\phi(L,L)}y_{3}=v_{\phi(L,L)}y_{6}=0
.

The following corollary is the type C analogue of
[
11
, Corollary 4.4]
.

Corollary 3.15
.

Suppose that
e(\bm{i})S^{\lambda}\neq 0
. Then

\{v\in e(\bm{i})S^{\lambda}\mid vy_{k}=0\text{ for all }k\in\{1,\dots,n\}\}=\mathcal{O}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i})}.

Proof.

Let
v=\sum_{\mathfrak{t}}a_{\mathfrak{t}}v_{\mathfrak{t}}\in e(\bm{i})S^{\lambda}
be such that
vy_{k}=0
for all
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Let
{\mathfrak{t}}^{*}=\phi_{\bm{i}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda,\bm{i})
be minimal in dominance order among those tableaux
{\mathfrak{t}}
with
a_{\mathfrak{t}}\neq 0
. Let
r_{1},r_{2},\dots,r_{A}\in\{1,\dots,d\}
be those integers such that
l_{r_{a}}=L
. For each
a\in\{1,\dots,A\}
, let
k_{a}\in\bm{s}^{(r_{a})}
be such that
\deg(y_{k_{a}}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=2
whenever
r_{a}\in W_{2}
and
\deg(y_{k_{a}}e(\bm{i}({\mathfrak{t}})))=4
if
r_{a}\in W_{4}
. Moreover, if
\bm{s}^{(r_{a})}\subset\bm{s}^{(r_{b})}
for some
a,b\in\{1,\dots,A\}
, then we further require that
k_{b}\notin\bm{s}^{(r_{a})}
. According to
section

3.1
, we have

(31)

vy_{k_{1}}y_{k_{2}}\dots y_{k_{A}}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i})}\neq 0.

It follows that the product of
y
’s must be empty and we have
v=a_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i})}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i})}
, as we claimed.
∎

It is particularly striking that the above result holds regardless of the characteristic of the ring
\mathcal{O}
.

3.2.
The
\psi
-action

In this section, we determine the right action of the quiver Hecke algebra generators
\psi_{r}
(
r\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
) on the Specht module basis given by
\{v_{\mathfrak{t}}\mid{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)\}
. The computation of
v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}
relies heavily on the relative positions of
r
and
r+1
in
{\mathfrak{t}}
, the standardness of the swapped tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}
, and the diagrammatic relations of the quiver Hecke algebra.

Our analysis naturally splits into distinct cases.

Theorem 3.16
.

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
and
r\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
. Let
i_{r}=\res({\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r))
and
i_{r+1}=\res({\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r+1))
.

(1)

If
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
and
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r+1)\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
, then

(32)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}}.

(2)

If
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r)\in\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
,
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r+1)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
, then

(33)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}}\mathcal{Q}_{i_{r},i_{r+1}}(y_{r},y_{r+1}).

(3)

Suppose
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r)
and
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r+1)
lie in the same component of
\lambda
(i.e., both in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
or both in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
), so that
{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}\notin\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
. Let
k=r+1,k^{\prime}=r
if the nodes are in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
, and
k=r,k^{\prime}=r+1
if they are in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
.

(a)

Suppose that there exists some
m\in\{1,\dots,d\}
such that
k\in\bm{s}^{(m)}
and
k\notin\bm{s}^{(m)}
, and furthermore
l_{m}=L
. Let
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
be the tableau having the same residue sequence as
{\mathfrak{t}}
and the same binary word, with the exception that
l_{m}=A
. The tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}s_{r}
is standard.

(i)

If
i_{k^{\prime}}\notin\{0,e\}
, then

(34)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}s_{r}}.

(ii)

Suppose instead that
i_{k^{\prime}}\in\{0,e\}
. Let
s
be the string of residue
i_{k}
crossing string
k+1
lowest down in the string diagram of
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Then

(35)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}s_{r}}(y_{k^{\prime}}+y_{s}).

(b)

Otherwise,

(36)

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=0.

Proof.

Case 1 is immediate. Since
r
and
r+1
lie in different components of the hook
\lambda
, the tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}
where the two numbers are swapped remains standard.

Case 2 is also clear. It can be evaluated further by using the description of the
y
-action in the previous section.

In Case 3,
{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}\notin\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
because
r
and
r+1
are adjacent in the same row or column. We will only cover the case where both nodes are in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
, as the other case is similar. When we multiply
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
by
\psi_{r}
, we add a crossing between strings
r
and
r+1
at the bottom of the diagram. The
\psi_{r}
crossing can slide upwards past the crossings of string
r
. Note that string
r
has residue
i_{r+1}
, since
\psi_{r}
exchanges the residues of strings
r
and
r+1
. If
\psi_{r}
reaches the top of the diagram without breaking any
(i_{r+1},i_{r+1})
-crossings, it evaluates to zero by the Specht relations, since
{\mathfrak{t}}s_{r}\notin\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
.

In order for the slide to yield a non-zero result, it must trigger the error term of the braid relations (
5
). This occurs exactly when the
\psi_{r}
-crossing slides past a string
s
such that the residues form the sequence
(i_{r+1},i_{r},i_{r+1})
. Diagrammatically, this condition matches exactly the condition for a dot
y_{r+1}
to break a crossing during the
y
-action on
v_{\mathfrak{t}}
. Consequently, there is a natural bijection between the broken diagrams produced by the
\psi_{r}
-slide and those produced by the
y_{r+1}
-slide. For this reason, we obtain
0
if
l_{m}=A
.

The key difference is that
\psi_{r}
exchanges strings
r
and
r+1
. In the
y
-action evaluation
v_{\mathfrak{t}}y_{r+1}=\sum c_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}
, the string
r+1
is moved from the leg to the arm in
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
. The
\psi_{r}
-action applies the same break with the difference that strings
r
and
r+1
now reach the arm and leg respectively, and that any term where both
r
and
r+1
are in the same double short residue sequence
\bm{s}^{(m)}
gives zero, as we see next.

As the
\psi_{r}
crossing between strings
r
and
r+1
(with residues
i_{r}
and
i_{r+1}
) slides upwards, it must pass through the other pair of strings in the double short residue sequence
\bm{s}^{(m)}
with residues
(i_{r},i_{r+1})
.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

If we look only at the part of the diagram above the red line, we obtain

i_{r}
u
i_{r+1}
i_{r+1}
i_{r}

Since
u
is immobile, the error term that resolves the
(i_{r+1},i_{r+1})
-crossing evaluates to zero. Therefore, we consider only the case where
r+1\in\bm{s}^{(m)},r\notin\bm{s}^{(m)}
for some
m
, as in the statement. Note that in this case
r
is at the start of such a sequence, and that furthermore there can only be one such sequence. In conclusion, if
i_{r}\notin\{0,e\}
, then

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}s_{r}}.

Otherwise, if
i_{r}\in\{0,e\}
, then the braid relation produces extra dots.

v_{\mathfrak{t}}\psi_{r}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}s_{r}}(y_{r}+y_{s}).

The action of the dots can be evaluated using the results in the previous section.
∎

Example 3.17
.

We continue
section

3.1
. Consider
\psi_{3}
acting on
\phi(L,L)
. The integers
3
and
4
are adjacent in the leg. Here
k=4
and
k^{\prime}=3
. Notice that
4\in\bm{s}^{(2)}
but
3\notin\bm{s}^{(2)}
, so
m=2
and
l_{2}=L
. Because
i_{3}=2=e
, this triggers the
(y_{k^{\prime}}+y_{s})=(y_{3}+y_{5})
relation. Flipping
l_{2}\to A
gives
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}=\phi(L,A)
. The action results in
v_{\phi(L,L)}\psi_{3}=\pm v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}s_{3}}(y_{3}+y_{5})
, producing the sum of two standard tableaux:

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{11.9139pt}{-45.57913pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
