\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

Our results on the action of the quiver Hecke algebra generating elements on Specht modules labelled by a partition having a short arm or a short leg are somewhat less wieldy than the corresponding lemmas in
[
11
]
, as is to be expected from type C computations. They can nevertheless be used to calculate the action (up to sign) on a basis element, and they will be useful as we begin our description of the homomorphisms into the Specht modules labelled by partitions of this kind.

4.
Homomorphisms when
\lambda
has a Short Leg

Let
\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})
with
b<2e
(short leg), and fix
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})
a partition of
n
. We say that a node
N\in[\mu]
is an
arm node
(resp.
leg node
) with respect to
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
if
{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N))\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
(resp. in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
). If we refer to arm or leg nodes of
\mu
without designating a specific tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
, then
{\mathfrak{t}}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})
is to be assumed.

Lemma 4.1
.

If there exists a non-zero
\varphi\in\Hom(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
, then
v\coloneq\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})
satisfies
v\in\mathcal{O}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
, and moreover,
\mu
can be split into a partition
[\mu_{L}]\subset[\mu]
and a skew partition
[\mu_{A}]=[\mu]\setminus[\mu_{L}]
so that
[\mu_{L}]
contains all leg nodes of
[\mu]
(and also the node
(1,1)
), and
[\mu_{A}]
contains all arm nodes of
[\mu]
.

Proof.

By
section

3.1
, we see that
v
satisfies
eq.

11
and the
y
-relation in
eq.

10
if and only if
v=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
.

Next, let
N=(c,d)
be an arm node, and suppose that
N^{\prime}=(c,d+1)
is a leg node, with
r\coloneq({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(N)
and
r+1=({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(N^{\prime})
. Then
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}\psi_{r}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})\sigma_{r}}\neq 0
. It follows that each row
[\mu_{j}]
can be split into a set
[\mu_{j}]_{L}
composed of leg nodes, followed by a set
[\mu_{j}]_{A}
composed of arm nodes.

Next, let
N=(a,b)
be an arm node, and suppose that
N^{\prime}=(a+1,b)
is a leg node. Due to the previous paragraph, each node in row
a
to the east of
N
is an arm node, and each node in row
b
to the west of
N^{\prime}
is a leg node. Therefore, the strings reaching the Garnir belt of
N
do not cross each other in
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
. The Garnir relation in
S^{\mu}
involves a linear combination of the terms in
\operatorname{Gar}^{N}
, which is taken to be zero. However, since the strings in the Garnir belt of
N
do not cross each other in
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
, each of the tableaux
{\mathfrak{t}}\in\operatorname{Gar}^{N}
is mapped to a basis element
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}\psi_{\mathfrak{t}}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})\sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}}
, since
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})\sigma_{\mathfrak{t}}
is standard. A linear combination of basis elements cannot yield zero, so that there cannot be a homomorphism sending
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}}
to
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
, which contradicts our assumptions.

It follows that each column
[\mu_{j}^{\prime}]
can be split into a set
[\mu_{j}^{\prime}]_{L}
composed of leg nodes, followed by a set
[\mu_{j}^{\prime}]_{A}
composed of arm nodes.

The lemma statement follows from the conclusions of the last two paragraphs.
∎

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
v\coloneq\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
. (That is, the constant
a
equals
1
in
\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
.) We consider two separate cases, according to whether the node
(1,2)
of
[\mu]
is an arm node or a leg node. The case where
(1,2)\notin[\mu]
fits without issue inside the case where
(1,2)
is an arm node of
[\mu]
, so we blend the two together into the case where
(1,2)
is
not a leg node
of
[\mu]
.

4.1.
The node
(1,2)
is not a leg node of
[\mu]

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
(1,2)
is not a leg node of
[\mu]
. Then the leg nodes of
\mu
are precisely
(2,1)
,
(3,1)
,
\dots
,
(b+1,1)
.

Proof.

This is a trivial consequence of
4.1
.
∎

For a partition
\nu\vdash b+1
such that
[\nu]\subseteq[\mu]
, we define
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(\nu)\in\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
to be the unique standard tableau obtained as follows:

(1)

For each node
N\in[\mu]\setminus[\nu]
, place the number
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N)
into
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
.

(2)

For each node
N\in[\nu]
, place the number
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N)
into
(1,1)\cup\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
.

According to
4.2
, if there is a homomorphism
\varphi
from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
where
(1,2)
is not a leg node of
[\mu]
and
\lambda
has a short leg, then (up to a constant)
\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(\nu)}
, where
\nu=(1^{b+1})
. Let us write simply
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
if
\nu=(1^{b+1})
. On the other hand, we also have (up to a constant)
\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
due to
4.1
. Next we give a condition under which
{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})}
and
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
match.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\mu
be a partition such that
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\lambda}_{\mu}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
. Then
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})
exists and

{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu}).

Proof.

We show that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})=\phi_{\bm{i}^{\mu}}^{\lambda}(A,\dots,A)
, which by
section

3.1
immediately implies
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}y_{k}=0
for all
k
.
Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}=\phi_{\bm{i}^{\mu}}^{\lambda}(l_{1},\dots,l_{d})
corresponds to a word with
l_{m}=L
for some
m\in\{1,\dots,d\}
. Let
\bm{s}^{(m)}\in DS(\bm{i}^{\mu})
be the associated interval of indices.

The choice
L
means that the integer
u=s^{(m)}_{1}
is placed in
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. By
4.2
, the node
N\coloneq({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(u)
lies in the first column of
[\mu]
, so
N=(r,1)
for some
r\geq 2
.
Because
\bm{s}^{(m)}
defines a double short residue sequence, the contiguous subset of integers
\bm{s}^{(m)}
must be partitioned equally into an Arm strip
S_{A}
and a Leg strip
S_{L}
within
\lambda
that generate identical residue sequences.
The nodes of
[\mu]
filled by the integers in
\bm{s}^{(m)}
consist of consecutive nodes starting in row
r
.

The leg nodes
S_{L}
are located in the first column of
\mu
, while the arm nodes
S_{A}
are located in arbitrary columns. The first arm node in
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{(m)})
is either in column
1
or
2
.

If it is in the second column, then it must be
N^{\prime}=(r,2)
, which is horizontally adjacent to
N=(r,1)
. For
S_{A}
and
S_{L}
to generate identical residue sequences, their starting nodes must have the same residue, but horizontally adjacent nodes in
\mu
have contents that differ by exactly 1, so their residues are adjacent in the type C quiver and can never be equal.

Assume instead that
N^{\prime}
is in the first column, so that
N^{\prime}=(r^{\prime},1)
. In that case all of
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{(m)})
is in the first column of
[\mu]
, and we have a double short residue sequence which is a subsequence of
\mathcal{I}
. This is also impossible.

This contradiction establishes that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
is constructed using only
A
choices, and therefore
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})
.
∎

We set up some additional simple notation. Given
c_{1}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{ce\mid c\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, we may write
\overline{c_{1}}^{+}
for
\overline{c_{1}}
if
\overline{c_{1}+1}=\overline{c_{1}}+1

Similarly, we may write
\overline{c_{1}}^{-}
for
\overline{c_{1}}
if
\overline{c_{1}+1}=\overline{c_{1}}-1
. The following is easy to check.

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{ce\mid c\in\mathbb{Z}\}
. Then

(1)

\overline{c_{1}}^{\pm}=\overline{c_{2}}^{\pm}
if and only if
c_{2}\equiv+c_{1}\mod 2e
, and

(2)

\overline{c_{1}}^{\pm}=\overline{c_{2}}^{\mp}
if and only if
c_{2}\equiv-c_{1}\mod 2e
.

The next lemma determines the shapes
\mu
for which
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
, which is a necessary condition for the existence of a homomorphism
\varphi\in\Hom_{R_{n}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
, as we have seen. The lemma is separated into a recursive and a non-recursive part.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})
be a partition of
n
such that
m\geq b+1
with charge
\kappa
and residue sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=\bm{i}^{\mu}
. Recall that
\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})
.

(1)

(a)

Suppose that
b>0
and
\mu_{1}=c(2e)
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then
\mu=(\mu_{1},\overline{\mu})
satisfies
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
if and only if
\overline{\mu}
satisfies
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\overline{\mu}}^{\overline{\lambda}}}=\bm{i}^{\overline{\mu}}
, where
\overline{\lambda}=(a+1-(\mu_{1}-1),1^{b-1})
with charge
\kappa-1
.

(b)

Suppose that
b=0
and
\mu_{1}=c(2e)-1
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then
\mu=(\mu_{1},\overline{\mu})
satisfies
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
if and only if
\overline{\mu}
satisfies
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\overline{\mu}}^{\overline{\lambda}}}=\bm{i}^{\overline{\mu}}
, where
\overline{\lambda}=(a+1-\mu_{1})
with charge
\kappa-1
.

(2)

Suppose that
\mu_{1}
is not as in the previous cases.
Then
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
if and only if
\mu
has one of the following shapes.

(a)

\mu=\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})

(b)

\mu=(a,1^{b+1})
and
a\equiv-b-1\mod 2e

(c)

\mu=(\mu_{1},1^{n-\mu_{1}})
and
b-\mu_{1}\equiv 2\kappa-1\mod 2e
.

(d)

\mu=(c(2e)-2\kappa,2^{y},1^{b-y})
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
y\in\{1,2,\dots,b\}
.

(e)

\mu=(c(2e)-2\kappa,2^{y},1^{b-y+1})
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
b-y\equiv 2\kappa-1\mod 2e
and
y\in\{1,2,\dots,b\}.

Proof.

Part
1
is a straightforward consequence of the fact that the set of contents
\{\kappa,\kappa+1,\dots,\kappa+c(2e)-1\}
maps to exactly
c
full cycles of the residues
\{0,1,\dots,e,e-1,\dots,1\}
. For instance, in (a), the residues of the nodes in the second row starting at node
(2,2)
fit also in the first row starting at
(1,c(2e)+1)
, and it follows that, if
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\overline{\mu}}^{\overline{\lambda}}}=\bm{i}^{\overline{\mu}}
(the definitions of
\overline{\lambda}
and
\overline{\mu}
are given in the statement), then a row with
c(2e)
nodes can be added above both
\overline{\lambda}
and
\overline{\mu}
, and further the nodes
(1,2),(1,3),\dots,(1,\overline{\lambda}_{1})
can be translated to the first row that was just added, to obtain
\lambda
and
\mu
, while preserving the correspondence of nodes
N\leftrightarrow({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}({\mathfrak{t}}_{\overline{\mu}}^{\overline{\lambda}}(N))
between
\overline{\lambda}
and
\overline{\mu}
in the new shapes
\lambda
and
\mu
, so that
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
also. The converse and the argument for (b) are both similar, and we skip the details.

For Part
2
, let the ordered nodes of
\mu\setminus\lambda
be
(M_{1},\dots,M_{p})
and those of
\lambda\setminus\mu
be
(L_{1},\dots,L_{p})
. Suppose first that
\mu
is a hook partition. If
\mu=\lambda
, the result holds trivially. Otherwise, it is necessary that
\overline{\cont{M_{1}}}=\overline{\cont{L_{1}}}
. In the case where
\overline{\cont{M_{1}}}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont{L_{1}}}^{\pm}
, it is easy to see that there cannot exist nodes
M_{2},L_{2}
, as otherwise

\overline{\cont{M_{2}}}=\overline{\cont{M_{1}}-1}\neq\overline{\cont{L_{1}}+1}=\overline{\cont{L_{2}}}.

The condition on the residues of
M_{1}
and
L_{1}
reduces to
a\equiv-b-1\mod 2e
. On the other hand, if
\overline{\cont{M_{1}}}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont{L_{1}}}^{\mp}
, then there may exist several nodes
(M_{1},\dots,M_{p})
and
(L_{1},\dots,L_{p})
, and the condition on the residues reduces to
b-\mu_{1}\equiv 2\kappa-1\mod 2e
.

Next, suppose instead that
\mu
is not a hook partition, so that
(2,2)=M_{1}\in[\mu]
. Since
\mu_{1}
is not a multiple of
2e
, we cannot have
\overline{\cont(M_{1})}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont(L_{1})}^{\pm}
, so it follows that
\overline{\cont(M_{1})}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont(L_{1})}^{\mp}
, and therefore, the residue of
(2,3)
is different from
\res(L_{2})
, if such a node exists. It follows that
(2,3)\notin[\mu]
, which shows that
[\mu]\setminus[\mu_{1}]
has exactly two columns. Moreover, the condition
\overline{\cont(M_{1})}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont(L_{1})}^{\mp}
reduces to
\mu_{1}\equiv-2\kappa\mod 2e
. The case (2.d) is now covered.

Under the conditions in (2.d), let
M_{y}
be the last node in the second column of
\mu
. If there is a node
M_{y+1}
below
(b+1,1)
in
[\mu]
, then we must have
\overline{\cont{M_{y+1}}}=\overline{\cont{M_{y}}-1}
. The condition that the leg of
\lambda
is short negates the possibility that
\overline{\cont{M_{y+1}}}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont{M_{y}}-1}^{\pm}
, so it follows that
\overline{\cont{M_{y+1}}}^{\pm}=\overline{\cont{M_{y}}-1}^{\mp}
, and this condition reduces to
b-y=2\kappa-1\mod 2e
. We have exhausted all possibilities for matching residues.
∎

Example 4.6
.

Let
\lambda=(13,1^{3}),\kappa=0,e=2
. We give a list of all partitions
\mu
satisfying
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
.

•

\mu=(13,1^{3})
, corresponding to
(2.a)
.

•

Set
\mu_{1}=12
, according to
(1.a)
. Then we have
\lambda^{\prime}=(2,1,1),\kappa^{\prime}=-1
.

–

\mu^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}=(2,1,1)
, corresponding to
(2.a)
, gives
\mu=(12,2,1,1)
.

–

\mu^{\prime}=(1^{4})
, corresponding to
(2.b)
, gives
\mu=(12,1^{4})
.

•

Set
\mu_{1}=8
, according to
(1.a)
. Then we have
\lambda^{\prime}=(6,1,1),\kappa^{\prime}=-1
.

–

\mu^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}=(6,1,1)
, corresponding to
(2.a)
, gives
\mu=(8,6,1,1)
.

–

\mu^{\prime}=(5,1,1,1)
, corresponding to
(2.b)
, gives
\mu=(8,5,1,1,1)
.

–

Set
\mu_{2}=4
, according to
(1.a)
. Then we have
\lambda^{\prime\prime}=(3,1),\kappa=-2
.

*

\mu^{\prime\prime}=\lambda^{\prime\prime}=(3,1)
, according to
(2.a)
, gives
\mu=(8,4,3,1)
.

*

\mu^{\prime\prime}=(2,1,1)
, according to
(2.b)
, gives
\mu=(8,4,2,1,1)
.

–

\mu^{\prime}=(1^{8})
, corresponding to
(2.c)
, gives
\mu=(8,1^{8})
.

•

Set
\mu_{1}=4
, according to
(1.a)
. Then we have
\lambda^{\prime}=(10,1,1),\kappa^{\prime}=-1
. The restriction that
\mu_{2}\leqslant\mu_{1}=4
is now relevant.

–

Set
\mu_{2}=4
, according to
(1.a)
. Then we have
\lambda^{\prime\prime}=(7,1),\kappa^{\prime\prime}=-2
. Further iterations lead to the partitions:

*

\mu=(4,4,4,4)

*

\mu=(4,4,4,3,1)

*

\mu=(4,4,2,1^{6})

–

\mu^{\prime}=(1^{12})
, corresponding to
(2.c)
, gives
\mu=(4,1^{12})
.

4.5
gives a collection of partitions
\mu
for which the residue relation in
eq.

11
and the
y
-relation in
eq.

10
allow for the existence of a homomorphism from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
. It is left to check the
\psi
- and Garnir relations.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
\mu
be a partition such that
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\lambda}_{\mu}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
. Then
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}
satisfies the
\psi
-relations of the Specht generator
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}}
given in
eq.

10
.

Proof.

Recall from
4.3
that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})
. For any two horizontally adjacent nodes
N,N^{\prime}\in[\mu]
filled with consecutive integers
k
and
k+1
in
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
, we must show that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi_{k}=0
.
We split into two cases based on the placement of
N
and
N^{\prime}
in
\mu
.

(1)

Both
N
and
N^{\prime}
are arm nodes of
[\mu]
:
In this case,
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
places both
k
and
k+1
in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
.
We apply
section

3.2
(Case 3). Since we have established that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}=\phi_{\bm{i}^{\mu}}^{\lambda}(A,\dots,A)
, we simply have
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi_{k}=0
.

(2)

N
is a Leg node and
N^{\prime}
is an arm node of
[\mu]
:
In this case,
({\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda})^{-1}(k+1)<({\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda})^{-1}(k)
, so that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi_{k}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}s_{k}}(y_{k}^{x_{1}}+y_{k+1}^{x_{2}})
, where
x_{1},x_{2}
are
1
or
2
depending on the residues of
N
and
N^{\prime}
, according to
section

3.2
. We have to check that the expression
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}s_{k}}(y_{k}^{x_{1}}+y_{k+1}^{x_{2}})
evaluates to zero.

Let
\bm{i}
be the residue sequence of
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
, let
\bm{i}^{\prime}=\bm{i}s_{k}
, and let
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}s_{k}
. Through the rest of the proof, we refer to arm and leg nodes of
[\mu]
with respect to their positions in
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
instead of
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
, which goes against our usual convention. Suppose, towards a contradiction, that
k
is at the end of a double short residue subsequence
\bm{s}^{\prime}\in DS(\bm{i}^{\prime})
. Let
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
be the subset of nodes inside
[\lambda]
filled with the numbers in
\bm{s}^{\prime}
in tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}
, and let
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
be the set of corresponding nodes in tableau
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
. Since
\bm{i}^{\prime}_{\bm{s}^{\prime}}
is a double short sequence of residues, it follows that for every arm node there must be a corresponding leg node with the same residue. Therefore, a leg node with the same residue as
N^{\prime}\in({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
must also be in
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
. Since leg nodes are limited to the first column of
[\mu]
, this requires that
N^{1}\in({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
, where
N^{1}
is the node immediately northwest of
N^{\prime}
. Next, the node
(N^{1})^{\prime}
immediately to the right of
N^{1}
and immediately above
N^{\prime}
satisfies
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N^{1})<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}((N^{1})^{\prime})<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N^{\prime})
, so it follows that
(N^{1})^{\prime}\in({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
. This in turn requires that
N^{2}\in({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
, where
N^{2}
is the node immediately to the northwest of
(N^{1})^{\prime}
, and this reasoning can be continued by induction. Since there are only finitely many rows in
[\mu]
, it follows that
k
cannot in fact be at the end of a double short residue subsequence
\bm{s}^{\prime}\in DS(\bm{i}^{\prime})
.

Next, suppose that
k+1
is at the start of a double short residue subsequence
\bm{s}^{\prime}\in DS(\bm{i}^{\prime})
. Let
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})\subset[\lambda]
be as in the previous paragraph. Since
i^{\prime}_{k+1}=\res(N)
, the first arm node in
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s})
, which we call
N^{\prime\prime}
, has the same residue in
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
. We reason differently according to where
N^{\prime\prime}
is located.

(a)

If the row starting with
N,N^{\prime}
has more nodes in it, then the node
N^{\prime\prime}
is immediately to the right of
N^{\prime}
. Since
\res(N)=\res(N^{\prime\prime})
, it follows that the node
N^{\prime}
has residue
0
or
e
, and therefore
x_{2}=2
, so the double crossing after
\psi_{k}
leads to a factor of
y_{k+1}^{2}
, and we have
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime}}y_{k+1}^{2}=0
, since
k+1
can be inside, at most, a single double short sequence
\bm{s}^{\prime}
with corresponding choice
L
.

(b)

Otherwise,
N^{\prime\prime}
is to the southeast of
N
, and the node
N_{1}
immediately to the south of
N
satisfies
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N)<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N_{1})<{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N^{\prime\prime})
, so that
N_{1}
must also be in
({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}(\bm{s}^{\prime})
, and then an induction similar to that in the previous paragraph may be pursued.

It follows from the previous two paragraphs that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi_{k}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}s_{k}}(y_{k}^{x_{1}}+y_{k+1}^{x_{2}})=0
, finishing the proof.
∎

We have proved that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}
satisfies the simple Specht relations given in
eq.

10
for all the shapes in
4.5
. Next we will find which of these shapes satisfy the Garnir relations given in
eq.

12
. The following computation will be useful to us.

.

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{7.4153pt}{62.23766pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{{}}{}{{}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 39.37 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

i_{1}\neq 0,1,e-1
or

and let

be characterised by the condition that
i_{1}=i_{k}
. Then we see that

Similar straightforward computations give the result in the case where
i_{1}=\text{$0$ or $e$}
and the case where
i_{1}=\text{$1$ or $e-1$}
.
∎

The next lemma in relation to Garnir relations is analogous to
[
11
, Lemma 5.7]
. The similarity of the statement is striking, though the computations are more involved in our case. The notation
(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
simply denotes a cycle of the symmetric group.

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
\mu
has one of the shapes in
4.5
and let
A=(x,y)\in\mu
be a Garnir node. Define
r,s,t
to be the values
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(x,y),{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(x+1,1)
and
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(x+1,y)
, respectively. Then

v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}=\begin{cases}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(t+1,t,\dots,s)}&\text{if $x\leq b$, $y\equiv 0\mod 2e$ and $y<\mu_{x+1}$},\\
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(r,r+1,\dots,s)}&\text{if $x\leq b$, $y\equiv 1\mod 2e$ and $y>1$},\\
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}&\text{if $x>b$ and $y\equiv 0\mod 2e$},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We take
j
to be the content of node
A=(x,y)
.

First we assume that
x\leq b
and
y\equiv 0\mod 2e
. Since
A
is a Garnir node it follows that
\mu_{x}\geq\mu_{x+1}\geq 2e
, and according to
4.5
we must have
\mu_{x}=c(2e)
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
. Consequently,
C^{A}
consists of a single node while
D^{A}
is empty. From this we may infer the structure of
\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}
. As in
[
11
]
, we obtain

(37)

Here
\char 12\relax={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(t,t-1,\dots,s)
is a standard tableau. Next, notice that the residue sequence of unfilled nodes of
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
in
\char 12\relax_{\downarrow(r-1)}
continues
(\overline{j},\overline{j+1},\overline{j+2},\dots)
, whereas the residue sequence of unfilled nodes of
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
continues
(\overline{j},\overline{j-1},\overline{j-2},\dots)
. This is a consequence of the assumption that
y\equiv 0\mod 2e
.
After we have placed the
c(2e)
numbers
r,r+1,\dots,t-1
into
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
in
\char 12\relax
, we again have that the residue sequence of unfilled nodes of
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
in
\char 12\relax_{\downarrow(t-1)}
continues
(\overline{j},\overline{j+1},\overline{j+2},\dots)
, whereas the residue sequence of unfilled nodes of
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
continues
(\overline{j},\overline{j-1},\overline{j-2},\dots)
.

It follows that, if string
t
starts a sequence
\bm{s}^{(r)}\in DS(\bm{i}(\char 12\relax))
, then
\bm{i}^{(r)}=(\overline{j},\overline{j})
. But
i_{t+1}(\bm{s})=\overline{j}
and
\char 12\relax^{-1}(t+1)\in\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
happen simultaneously if and only if
y<\mu_{x+1}
. In that case,
y_{t}
simply breaks the crossing between
t
and
t+1
as described in
section

3.1
and we obtain
v_{\char 12\relax}y_{t}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(t+1,t,\dots,s)}
. Otherwise, if
y=\mu_{x+1}
, then
v_{\char 12\relax}y_{t}=0
.

Next we let
x\leq b
and
y\equiv 1\mod 2e
. We first assume that
x\leq b
and
y=1
. If
x=1
, then
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}=0
due to the Garnir relation for the node
(1,1)
. Otherwise both
A=(x,1)
and
A^{\prime}\coloneq(x+1,1)
are leg nodes, since
x\leq b
.

If
\mu_{x}=1
, then
\mu_{x+1}=1
also. The element
\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}
in this case is simply
\psi_{r}
, and we have
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi_{r}=0
according to case 3 of
section

3.2
, since
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}={\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})
, that is, the binary word of
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
is composed only of
A
’s.

Suppose instead that
\mu_{x}>1
. In that case, the node
A^{\prime\prime}=(x,2)
with residue
\overline{j+1}
is an arm node. Following Loubert, we have

(39)

Let
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime\prime}
be the (standard) tableau obtained from the diagram above by substituting the bottom braid relation by the Error term (and ignoring the dots). Then the number
r+2
cannot start a designated sequence in
{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime\prime}
, because when
r+2
is placed, the addable residue in the arm is either
0
or
2
, while the residue of
r+2
is
1
. It follows that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\prime\prime}}y_{r+2}=0
, so that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}=0
.

, we have
\mu_{x}=c(2e)
for some

. It follows that both
C^{A}
and
D^{A}
have at least two nodes and, as in
[
11
]
, we reach a diagram which has a subdiagram at the top as follows.

.

Next we check the special case
A=(b+1,1)
. If
\mu_{b+1}=1
, then the Garnir relation involves a single
\psi
, which gives a double crossing when applied to
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}
. This gives zero due to arguments like those in the proof of
4.7
. Next, note that we cannot have
\mu_{b+1}=2
, as nodes
N^{\prime},N^{\prime\prime}\in[\mu]
respectively to the immediate east and south of node
N
do not have adjacent residues, and so they cannot both be consecutive arm nodes. Suppose instead that
\mu_{b+1}\geqslant 3
. Following Loubert, we obtain

v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}=\hbox to74.98pt{\vbox to83.23pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.19046pt\lower-14.44379pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

This diagram gives zero: the crossing
\chi_{r,s}
between strings
r
and
s
can slide up past the crossing
\chi_{r+1,s}
. The Slide term (according to
eq.

5
) gives zero, and the Error term (if it exists) gives zero due to the leftover crossing
\chi_{r+1,s-1}
, which triggers a
\psi
-relation.

Next we check the case where
x>b
and
y\not\equiv 0\mod 2e
, while also
y\neq 1
. In this case, every node
A^{\prime}
such that
A^{\prime}>A
is an arm node, and
C^{A}\neq\varnothing
, so that
\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}
is a non-trivial element. Recall that all leg nodes before
r
are immobile, as they are followed by an arm node which immobilizes them. Therefore, any
\psi_{m}
for
m\geqslant r
annihilates
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}
, so that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}=0
.

Finally, if
x>b
and
y\equiv 0\mod 2e
, then according to
4.5
, row
x
has
c(2e)-1
nodes, so that
C^{A}=D^{A}=\varnothing
. It follows that
{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}={\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}
, so that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}\psi^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{A}}=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}
.
∎

If
\mu_{k}
is a row of
\mu
, we write
\mu_{k}^{A}
for the number of Arm nodes in
\mu_{k}
, which is either
\mu_{k}
or
\mu_{k}-1
. The
ceiling
2e
-quotient

{\bar{\mu}}
of a partition
\mu
is given by

{\bar{\mu}}_{k}=\left\lceil\frac{\mu_{k}^{A}+1}{2e}\right\rceil

The next lemma follows in the same way as
[
11
, Lemma 5.9]
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Under the same conditions as the previous lemma, and for any
1\leqslant m\leqslant\bar{\mu}_{x}
, the following holds.

v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}g^{A}=\begin{cases}\binom{\bar{\mu}_{x}}{m}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(t+1,t,\dots,s)}&\text{if $x\leq b$, $A=(x,m(2e))$ and $\mu_{x+1}>m(2e),$}\\
\binom{\bar{\mu}_{x}}{m}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}(r,r+1,\dots,s)}&\text{if $x\leq b$, $A=(x,m(2e)+1)$ and $m>0,$}\\
\binom{\bar{\mu}_{x}}{m}v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}&\text{if $x>b$ and $A=(x,m(2e))$, and}\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Now the main result of the section follows just as it does in
[
11
]
.
We borrow the following piece of notation. Given a partition
\nu
, we define the
Garnir content
of
\nu
as follows.

\Gc(\nu)=\Gc(\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m})=\gcd\left\{\binom{\nu_{i}}{k}\mid 1\leqslant k\leqslant\nu_{i+1}-1,\ 1\leqslant i\leqslant m-1\right\}.

We use the convention that
\gcd(\varnothing)=0
.

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
\lambda,\mu
be partitions of
n
such that
\lambda
is a hook with a short leg, and consider only homomorphisms where
(1,2)
is not a leg node of
[\mu]
. Then there exists a non-zero
\varphi\in\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
if and only if
\mu
is one of the shapes in
4.5
with ceiling
2e
-quotient
\bar{\mu}
satisfying
\Gc(\bar{\mu})=0
.

Example 4.12
.

Most partitions in
section

4.1
lead to homomorphisms. The exceptions are
\mu^{1}=(8,6,1,1)
and
\mu^{2}=(8,5,1,1,1)
. We have
\bar{\mu}^{1}=(2,2,1,1)
and
\bar{\mu}^{2}=(2,2,1,1,1)
. Both cases lead to
\Gc(\bar{\mu})=2
, so that the corresponding homomorphisms only exist in characteristic
2
.

4.2.
The node
(1,2)
is a leg node of
[\mu]

Suppose that there is a non-zero homomorphism
\varphi\in\Hom(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
, and let
{\mathfrak{t}}\coloneq{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mathrm{max}}(\bm{i}^{\mu})
so that
\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})=v_{{\mathfrak{t}}}
. In this subsection we assume that
(1,2)
is a leg node of
[\mu]
. In particular, we must have
\overline{\kappa-1}=\overline{\kappa+1}
, so that the charge
\kappa
must be
0
or
e
, and without loss of generality we work in
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}
.

Lemma 4.13
.

The partition
[\mu]
must be a hook.

Proof.

Suppose that
[\mu]
is not a hook. Then
[\mu]
contains the node
N=(2,2)
.
This must be an arm node of
[\mu]
, because
N
has residue zero and
[\lambda]
has a short leg and charge zero.

Now, let
r\coloneq{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}(N)
and
N^{\prime}\coloneq{\mathfrak{t}}^{-1}(r)\in[\lambda]
.
Then
N^{\prime}
is separated from the corner node
(1,1)
by at least
2e-1
nodes, since they both have residue zero, and in particular the two nodes
A,A^{\prime}\in[\lambda]
immediately to the right of
(1,1)
in
[\lambda]
have residues
1
and
2
. Let
\bar{A}=({\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu})^{-1}({\mathfrak{t}}(A))
and define
\bar{A}^{\prime}
similarly. Then
\bar{A}
and
\bar{A}^{\prime}
are the first two arm nodes in
[\mu]
.

It follows that the node
B\in[\mu]
immediately to the left of
\bar{A}
, with residue
0
, is a leg node. But
(1,2)
is also a leg node, so
A\neq(1,2)
, and therefore
B\neq(1,1)
. We have reached a contradiction, as both
(1,1)
and
B
are leg nodes of
[\mu]
with residue
0
, despite the fact that
[\lambda]
has a short leg.
∎

Lemma 4.14
.

The length of
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
must be strictly larger than the length of
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
.

Proof.

Suppose that the length of
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
is at least the same as the length of
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. In that case, there must exist
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 2}
such that
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\downarrow k}
has the same amount of nodes in the arm as in the leg, as
(1,2)
is a leg node. This implies that the sequence
(i_{2},\dots,i_{k})
is a double short residue sequence. Since the number
2
goes to the leg, it follows from
section

3.1
that there must be a
k^{\prime}
such that
vy_{k^{\prime}}\neq 0
, which contradicts our assumption that
v_{\mathfrak{t}}=\varphi(v_{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\mu}})
.
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
\lambda,\mu
be partitions of
n
such that
\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})
is a hook with a short leg, and consider only homomorphisms where
(1,2)
is a leg node of
[\mu]
, so that
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}
or
\Lambda_{e}
. Then there exists a non-zero
\varphi\in\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})
if and only if one of the following holds.

(1)

\mu=(b+1,1^{a})
, where
b>a
.

(2)

\mu=(2e)
and
\lambda=(2,1^{2e-2})
.

(3)

\mu=(2e-1,1)
and
\lambda=(1^{2e})
.

Proof.

First we establish the shape of
\mu
according to the possible residue sequences. Recall that we have the condition
(1,2)\in[\mu_{L}]
. Let us split our investigation according to whether
(2,1)\in[\mu_{L}]
or
(2,1)\notin[\mu_{L}]
.

(1)

Suppose first that
(2,1)\in[\mu_{L}]
. Since we have charge
0
, it follows that
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
must have the residue sequence
(1,2,\dots,1)
with length
2e-1
, so that
b=2e-1
. In this case, the partition
\mu_{L}
must have
\bm{i}^{\mu_{L}}=(0,1,2,\dots,1)
, so that
\mu_{L}=(2e-1,1)
. In this case, the shape
[\mu_{A}]
(if it is non-empty) must have a single node in
\operatorname{Arm}(\mu)
and can continue in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mu)
. In this case we have
\mu=(b+1,1^{a})
.

(2)

Suppose that
(2,1)\notin[\mu_{L}]
. In this case we simply have
\mu_{L}=(b+1)
.

(a)

If
b\neq 2e-2
, then the first residue
1
in the arm of
\lambda
differs from the next addable node in the arm of
\mu_{L}
, so that
[\mu_{A}]
must be confined to the leg of
\mu
. In this case we have
\mu=(b+1,1^{a})
.

(b)

If
b=2e-2
, then
[\mu_{A}]
may confined to the leg as before, but it is also possible that
[\mu_{A}]
is a single node in the arm of
[\mu]
. In this case,
\mu
is the trivial partition
(2e)
.

In the case
\mu=(b+1,1^{a})
with
b=2e-1
and
a\geqslant 1
, there are two candidates for
\mu_{L}
, that is, we may have
\mu_{L}=(2e-1,1)
or
\mu_{L}=(2e)
. In fact, we must have
\mu_{L}=(2e-1,1)
. Indeed, if we had
\mu_{L}=2e
and
a\neq 0
, then the strings
2e
and
2e+1
cross in
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}
and they share the same residue, so that
v_{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}y_{2e}\neq 0
.

In each case, the Garnir relation for the node
(1,1)
is easily seen to hold, as all tableaux in
\operatorname{Std}(\lambda)
have residue sequence starting with 0. The
y
-relations also hold, since the tableaux
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
obtained from the above
[\mu_{L}]
and
[\mu_{A}]
can be checked to be maximal for their respective residue sequences. The
\psi
-relations (and the Garnir relations for nodes in
\operatorname{Leg}(\mu)
, which also involve a single
\psi
-element) hold in each case due to cases (2) and (3) in
section

3.2
, since the
y
-action is zero.
∎

4.3.
The trivial module

For reference, we give a version of
section

4.1
in the special case of the trivial module
S^{\lambda}=S^{(n)}
.

Lemma 4.16
.

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})
be a partition of
n
such that
m\geq 1
with charge
\kappa
and residue sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})=\bm{i}^{\mu}
. Let
\lambda=(n)
.

(1)

Suppose that
\mu_{1}=c(2e)-1
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
. Then
\mu=(\mu_{1},\overline{\mu})
satisfies
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
if and only if
\overline{\mu}
satisfies
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\overline{\mu}}^{\overline{\lambda}}}=\bm{i}^{\overline{\mu}}
, where
\overline{\lambda}=(n-\mu_{1})
with charge
\kappa-1
.

(2)

Suppose that
\mu_{1}
is not as in the previous case.
Then
\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}}=\bm{i}^{\mu}
if and only if
\mu
has one of the following shapes.

(a)

\mu=\lambda=(n)

(b)

\mu=(\mu_{1},1^{n-\mu_{1}})
and
-\mu_{1}\equiv 2\kappa-1\mod 2e
.

Theorem 4.17
.

Let
\lambda,\mu
be partitions of
n
such that
\lambda=(n)
. Then there exists a non-zero
\varphi\in\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda}}(S^{\mu},S^{(n)})
if and only if
\mu
is one of the shapes in
4.16
with ceiling
2e
-quotient
\bar{\mu}
satisfying
\Gc(\bar{\mu})=0
.

Example 4.18
.

Let
\lambda=(12),\kappa=0
.
The list of partitions
\mu
satisfying the condition in
4.16
can be computed as in
section

4.1
. Here we simply give the full list of partitions. These are
(12)
,
(11,1)
,
(9,1,1,1)
,

(7,5)
,

(7,3,2)
,

(7,3,1,1)
,

(5,1^{7})
,

(3,3,3,3)
,

(3,3,1^{6})
and

(1^{12})
.
Of these, all of them have
Gc(\bar{\mu})=0
except for
\mu^{*}=(7,5)
, for which
\Gc(\bar{\mu}^{*})=\Gc(2,2)
= 2. Therefore, all of the corresponding homomorphisms exist in any characteristic, except for the partition
\mu^{*}
. In that case
\Hom_{R_{n}^{\Lambda_{0}}}(S^{\mu^{*}},S^{\lambda})\neq 0
only over a ring of characteristic
2
.

4.4.
Connection between types A and C

In
[
7
, Section 4]
we found that the homomorphism spaces between Specht modules over different quiver Hecke algebras of type A (varying the quantum characteristic) are sometimes related.

Our results on homomorphisms between Specht modules of type
C_{e}^{(1)}
suggest a further connection. Let us check our main result,
section

4.1
, against the results for homomorphisms to hooks in type
A_{2e-1}^{(1)}
. It is striking that the shapes given in
[
11
, Main Theorem]
are precisely the shapes that appear in
section

4.1
if we restrict our attention to the partitions that arise from (2.a) and (2.b) in
4.5
. Let us investigate an example.

Example 4.19
.

Let
\lambda=(4,1^{3})
, and let us look at the partitions
\mu
such that the homomorphism space is non-zero under quiver Hecke algebras
R_{n}({\Gamma})
for different quivers
{\Gamma}
.

(1)

If
\Gamma=A_{3}^{(1)}
, then the only homomorphism is the identity, with
\mu=(4,1^{3})
.

(2)

If
\Gamma=C_{2}^{(1)}
, then we can split our search depending on the charge:

(a)

If the charge is
0
or
2
, then again the non-zero homomorphism is the identity, and
\mu=(4,1^{3})
.

(b)

If the charge is
1
, then the following yield homomorphisms.

•

\mu=(4,1^{3})

•

\mu=(2^{3},1)

•

\mu=(2,1^{6})

(3)

If
\Gamma=A_{1}^{(1)}
, then several partitions yield homomorphisms, as follows.

•

\mu=(6,1)

•

\mu=(4,3)

•

\mu=(4,1^{3})

•

\mu=(2^{3},1)

•

\mu=(2,1^{6})

The partition in 1 appears in both (2.a) and (2.b), and each partition in (2.a) or (2.b) appears in 3. The above hold for an arbitrary field
k
, but there exist other examples where the characteristic of
k
is relevant.

We extrapolate our findings to all partitions
\mu,\lambda
.

Conjecture 4.20
.

Let
R_{n}(\Gamma,p)
be the quiver Hecke algebra corresponding to quiver
\Gamma
over a field of characteristic
p
. Let
\mu,\lambda\vdash n
be arbitrary partitions.

(1)

Let
e\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 2}
. Whenever

\Hom_{R_{n}(A_{2e-1}^{(1)},p)}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq\{0\},

we also find that:

\Hom_{R_{n}(C_{e}^{(1)},p)}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq\{0\}\quad\text{(for any charge $\kappa$).}

(2)

Whenever

\Hom_{R_{n}(C_{2}^{(1)},2)}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq\{0\}\quad\text{(for any charge $\kappa$)},

we also find that

\Hom_{R_{n}(A_{1}^{(1)},2)}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq\{0\}.

This conjecture suggests a structure of quiver specialization, where a generic parametrized quiver specializes to a quiver with fewer vertices under certain values of the parameter. This structure was suggested to the first author by Ben Webster. It is likely that the results in
[
7
, Section 4]
, and perhaps the first part of
section

4.4
, follow from
[
14
, Theorem 6.4]
, while the second part may require further investigation.

5.
Homomorphisms when
\lambda
has a Short Arm

Let
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{m})
and
\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})
with
a<2e
be two partitions of
n
.
In this section, as in the previous one, we investigate homomorphisms from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
, but now we restrict the amount of nodes in
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
instead of
\operatorname{Leg}(\lambda)
. The homomorphisms to
S^{\lambda}
under the condition that
\operatorname{Arm}(\lambda)
be short (that is, it has less than
2e
nodes) also send the Specht module generator to a single basis element, as
section

3.1
can also be applied in this case.

In the short arm case, the case where the node
(1,2)\in[\mu]
is not a leg node becomes simpler, while the case where
(1,2)
is a leg node becomes significantly harder. We quickly cover the simpler case, and we discuss the difficulties in the harder case by going over two examples.

5.1.
The node
(1,2)
is not a leg node of
[\mu]

Recall from
section

4.2
that this case covers every homomorphism whenever
\Lambda\neq\Lambda_{0}
or
\Lambda_{e}
. Applying
4.1
, we see that all of
\mu
’s leg nodes appear in its first column. Define
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
as in the previous section (before
4.3
).

Lemma 5.1
.

We have that
\bm{i}^{\mu}=\bm{i}^{{\mathfrak{t}}^{\lambda}_{\mu}}
if and only if
\mu
is one of the following partitions:

(1)

\mu=\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})

(2)

\mu=(a,1^{b+1})
and
a\equiv-b-1\mod 2e

(3)

\mu=(\mu_{1},1^{n-\mu_{1}})
and
b-\mu_{1}\equiv 2\kappa-1\mod 2e
.

(4)

\mu=(c(2e)-2\kappa,2^{y},1^{b-y})
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
y\in\{1,2,\dots,b\}
.

(5)

\mu=(c(2e)-2\kappa,2^{y},1^{b-y+1})
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
b-y\equiv 2\kappa-1\mod 2e
and
y\in\{1,2,\dots,b\}
.

Proof.

Similar to the proof of
4.5
, with the single simplification that the arm of
\lambda
is short, which eliminates the possibility of
\mu_{1}
having a multiple of
2e
nodes, and also the possibility of
\mu_{1}
having
c(2e)-1
nodes, except in the case
c=1
and
\mu=\lambda
, which we already cover.
∎

It is left to check which of the above
{\mathfrak{t}}_{\mu}^{\lambda}
satisfy the Specht module relations of
S^{\mu}
. In fact, they are satisfied
for all
the shapes above, as one can see by retracing the proofs of
4.7
and
4.9
, which are slightly simpler in the current case, as the possible shapes of
\mu
are comparatively restricted.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\lambda=(a+1,1^{b})
be a partition with a short arm. Then there is a homomorphism from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
such that
(1,2)\notin[\mu_{L}]
if and only if the partition
\mu
has one of the shapes appearing in
5.1
.

5.2.
The node
(1,2)
is a leg node of
[\mu]

As in
section

4.2
, this case can only present if
\Lambda=\Lambda_{0}
or
\Lambda_{e}
. It is significantly more difficult to analyze, and we do not give a full classification. According to
section

3.1
, the nodes in
[\mu]
can be separated into
[\mu_{L}]
and
[\mu_{A}]
, but in this case the subset
[\mu_{L}]
is not restricted to the first column of
[\mu]
. This greatly increases the possibilities for partitions
\mu
with potential Specht module homomorphisms to
S^{\lambda}
.

Example 5.3
.

Let
e=3,\kappa=(0),\lambda=(4,1^{5})
. Then the partitions
\mu
such that there is a homomorphism from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
are as follows:

•

\mu=(2,1^{7})
gives a homomorphism where
(1,2)\in[\mu]
is an arm node.

•

\mu=(4,1^{5})
gives the identity homomorphism.

•

\mu=(4,3,1,1)

•

\mu=(4,3,2)

•

\mu=(6,1,1,1)

•

\mu=(8,1)

Let us focus on the case
\mu=(4,3,2)
. The homomorphism sends the initial tableau

We see that, since we no longer have an analog of
4.2
, we lack control on
\mu_{L}
, and as a result, in general we obtain many more candidates for the partition
\mu
.

Example 5.4
.

Let
e=3,\kappa=(0),\lambda=(2,1^{8})
. One candidate for a homomorphism is
\mu=(5,3,2)
, since the choice
\mu_{L}=(5,3,1)
gives the correct residue sequence. We fill in the nodes with residues and shade the nodes in our candidate
[\mu_{L}]
(which we call
\nu
) in blue.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\neq 0.

It follows that there is no non-zero homomorphism from
S^{\mu}
to
S^{\lambda}
. In
section

4
, we saw that whenever there is a candidate
\mu
with the correct residue sequence, there is a homomorphism unless the Garnir relations prevent it. However, this no longer holds in the short arm case, as we have just seen.

The last two examples show that care is needed when enumerating the homomorphisms to a hook
\lambda
with a short arm. On the one hand, there are more candidate source partitions
\mu
. On the other hand, some plausible partitions do not produce a homomorphism. A full description of the partitions
\mu
such that
\Hom_{R_{n}}(S^{\mu},S^{\lambda})\neq\{0\}
in the spirit of
section

4.1
would therefore be significantly harder to achieve, but we do not expect it to be outside the scope of the methods used in this text.
